S.4
Additional notation used in the proofs

Given
a\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}
, we use direct product notation for their concatenation, so that
a\oplus b:=[a^{\top},b^{\top}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}+n_{2}}
, and we will frequently use this operation iteratively; for example if
\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\}
is a collection of 3 elements of
\mathbb{R}^{M}
,
\oplus_{k=1}^{3}x_{k}=(x_{1}\oplus x_{2})\oplus x_{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{3M}
. If for all
1\leq k\leq K
,
x_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
, we define
\pi_{\ell}[\oplus_{k=1}^{K}x_{k}]:=x_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
, so that
\pi_{\ell}
stands for projection of a vector
y\in(\mathbb{R}^{M})^{K}
onto the
\ell^{th}
copy of
\mathbb{R}^{M}
. Following
Oksendal 2013
we frequently use the notation
(\mathbb{R}^{M})^{K}
to mean
KM
dimensional Euclidean space, rather than
\mathbb{R}^{MK}
, to emphasize a particular decomposition of the vector space. Given independent random variables
X
and
Y
, and a measurable real valued function
h
, we define
{\mathrm{P}}_{X}(h(X,Y)\leq t):=g(Y)
, where
g(y)=\mathbb{E}[{\mathrm{P}}(h(X,y)\leq t)]:=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}\{h(X,y)\leq t\}]
. Given random elements
X
and
Y
taking values in the same measure space,
X\overset{d}{=}Y
means that
{\mathrm{P}}(X\in B)={\mathrm{P}}(Y\in B)
for all measurable subsets
B
of the space, and for general random elements
X
and
Y
the notation
X\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}Y
means that
X
and
Y
are stochasticallly independent. If
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
are
\sigma
-fields, the notation
\mathcal{F}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}\mathcal{G}
means that
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
are independent
\sigma
-fields. By
\sigma(X)
we mean the
\sigma
-field generated by
X
. If
S
is a topological space, the notation
\mathcal{B}_{S}
refers to the Borel
\sigma
-field on
S
. For a real number
x
we define
x_{+}:=\max\{x,0\}
. Given real numbers
a_{1},\dots a_{n}
,
\textrm{diag}[a_{1},\dots a_{n}]\in M_{n\times n}
is defined entry-wise by the equation
[\textrm{diag}[a_{1},\dots a_{n}]]_{ij}=\delta_{ij}a_{i}
, where
\delta_{ij}=1
if
i=j
and
0
if
i\neq j
. Given
a\in\mathbb{R}
, we define
\lfloor a\rfloor=\sup\{z\in\mathbb{Z},z\leq a\}
, and
\lceil a\rceil=\inf\{z\in\mathbb{Z},z\geq a\}
, i.e. the floor and ceiling functions. If
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are independent random elements satisfying
X_{i}\overset{d}{=}X_{j}
for all
1\ \leq i\leq n
and all
1\leq j\leq n
, we say that
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are an
i.i.d.
collection or are
i.i.d.
. If
a
and
b
are real numbers we define
a\vee b:\max\{a,b\}
, and
a\wedge b:=\min\{a,b\}
. If
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\|v\|_{2}:=\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{n}v_{j}^{2}}
,
\|v\|_{1}:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}|v_{j}|
, and
\|v\|_{\infty}:=\max_{j=1}^{n}|v_{j}|
, and these definitions are extended to matrices by identifying the matrix with a vector. We define
\exp
to be the exponential function. For
f
and
g
real valued functions (with domain
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{N}
, etc.) the notation
f\lesssim g
means that there is a universal constant
C
such that
f\leq Cg
. Let
I=[0,1]\subset\mathbb{R}
, unless otherwise stated. If
A
is a finite set, we denote by
|A|
the number of elements contained in
A
. If
A\subset\mathbb{R}
is an interval, we denote by
|A|
the number
\sup A-\inf A
.

We now introduce notation that is used in section
S.5
, which contains proofs of Theorem
1
and
2
. The notation also appears in section
S.6
when specializing our general technical results into a form that directly applies to the proof arguments made in section
S.5
. Recall the definition of the test statistic
T_{n}^{k}
(defined for all
1\leq k\leq K
), the global test statistic
T_{n}=\max_{k=1}^{K}T_{n}^{k}
, the bootstrap statistic
T_{n}(e)
, and the empirical c.d.f
\widehat{F}(t)
, all defined in Section
2.2
. Recall the notation introduced in Assumptions
1
. Let
\Phi_{i,k}:=\oplus_{j=1}^{M}\phi_{j}^{\widetilde{f}_{k,i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
and
\Phi_{i}:=\oplus_{k=1}^{K}\Phi_{i,k}\in\mathbb{R}^{MK}
and
\Phi:=\oplus_{i=1}^{n}\Phi_{i}
. Let
\Sigma^{\Phi}
denote the covariance matrix of
n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Phi_{i}
. Let
\widehat{\Sigma}^{\Phi}
denote the empirical covariance of
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}
and let
\widehat{\Sigma}^{\Phi}_{k}
denote the empirical covariance of
\pi_{k}\Phi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k}\Phi_{n}
. Let
g
be a Gaussian in
(\mathbb{R}^{M})^{K}
with
\Cov(g)=\Sigma^{\Phi}
and let
e_{i},i=1,\dots,n
denote
i.i.d.
standard Gaussians with
e=(e_{1},\dots,e_{n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
independent of
\Phi
. Define

\displaystyle{T^{d}(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})}

\displaystyle:=\max_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f^{k}_{i}(t_{\ell})\Big)^{2},

(27)

\displaystyle T^{M}(\Phi)

\displaystyle:=\max_{k=1}^{K}\Big\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Phi_{i,k}\Big\|_{2}^{2},

(28)

\displaystyle T^{M,G}(g)

\displaystyle:=\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}g\|_{2}^{2},

(29)

\displaystyle T^{M,e}(\Phi,e)

\displaystyle:=\max_{k=1}^{K}\Big\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}(\Phi_{i,k}-\overline{\Phi}_{\cdot,k})\Big\|_{2}^{2},

(30)

\displaystyle{T^{d,e}(f_{1},\dots,f_{n},e)}

\displaystyle:=\max_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[f^{k}_{i}(t_{\ell})-\bar{f}^{k}_{\cdot}(t_{\ell})]\Big)^{2}.

(31)

In many cases we will lighten notation by omitting the arguments of
{T^{d}},T^{M},T^{M,G},T^{M,e},{T^{d,e}}
above. Let
E=\oplus_{i=1}^{N}e^{i}
for i.i.d.
N(0,I_{n})
random vectors
e^{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, independent of
\Phi,g
, and let
t\mapsto\widehat{F}(t;f,E)
denote the empirical cdf of the collection
\{T_{n}(e^{1}),\dots,T_{n}(e^{N})\}
, and let
\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}
be the
1-\gamma
empirical quantile of the collection. Sometimes we will write
\widehat{F}(t)
instead of
\widehat{F}(t;f,E)
to lighten notation. With this notation

\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}:=\inf\{s\in\mathbb{R}:1-\gamma\leq\widehat{F}(s)\},

which is equal to the
\lceil(1-\gamma)N\rceil
order statistic of the collection
\{T_{n}(e^{1}),\dots,T_{n}(e^{N})\}
. Let
\sigma(f)
be the
\sigma
-field generated by the random element
f\in((C[I])^{K})^{n}
.

S.5
Proofs

S.5.1
Proof of Theorem
1

Note that

\displaystyle\mbox{FWER}_{\gamma}

\displaystyle={\mathrm{P}}\Big(\exists k\in\{1,\dots,K\}\mbox{ such that }\mu^{k}\equiv 0,1-\widehat{F}(T^{k}_{n})<\gamma\Big)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\exists k\in\{1,\dots,K\}\penalty\ :\penalty\ 1-\widehat{F}(T^{k}_{n})<\gamma\mbox{ when }\mu^{j}\equiv 0\penalty\ \forall j=1,\dots,K\Big),

where the final term in the above display is the probability of one false rejection under the global null hypothesis
H^{global}_{0}
. Thus (
11
) is implied by (
12
). Since it suffices to prove (
12
) we assume throughout the proof that
H^{global}_{0}
holds. In particular we assume that for all
1\leq k\leq K
we have
\mu^{k}\equiv 0
, i.e that the continuous function
\mu^{k}
is identically equal to 0 and so for all
1\leq i\leq n
and all
1\leq k\leq K
,
f^{k}_{i}
is centered and therefore
\widetilde{f}\overset{d}{=}f
. Note that

\Big|\mbox{FWER}_{\gamma}-\gamma\Big|=\Big|1-\gamma-{\mathrm{P}}\Big(T_{n}\leq\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}\Big)\Big|.

(32)

Observe that
T^{d}(f)=T_{n}
(see (
3
)) and
T^{d,e}(f,e)=T_{n}(e)
(see (
4
)) almost surely. We will thus work with
T^{d},T^{d,e}
in what follows (for example, the empirical quantile
\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}
may be derived from the collection
\{T^{d,e}(f,e^{1}),\dots,T^{d,e}(f,e^{N})\}
).
By (
32
) it suffices to show that
under (A1) – (A3),

|{\mathrm{P}}(T^{d}\leq\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma})-(1-\gamma)|\leq C_{1-\gamma}d(n,K,T,n),

(33)

and under (A1)—(A4),

|{\mathrm{P}}(T^{d}\leq\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma})-(1-\gamma)|\leq\widetilde{C}_{1-\gamma}\widetilde{d}(n,K,T,n),

(34)

for constants
C_{1-\gamma}
and
\widetilde{C}_{1-\gamma}
that depend on the distribution of the data-generating distribution only through the model parameters appearing in (A1)-(A3) and (A1)-(A4) respectively.
We start by collecting several preliminary results. Let
q_{X}(\cdot)
denote the quantile function of a generic random variable
X
, and recall that for any
\gamma
,
F_{X}(q_{X}(\gamma))=\gamma
if
F_{X}(\cdot)
is a continuous function.

Recall that under Assumption (A3) there exist
k^{*},j^{*}
such that
\Var(g^{f_{k^{*}}}_{j^{*}})\geq v_{-}>0
. Then we have almost surely

T^{M,G}=\max_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{M}(g^{f_{k}}_{j})^{2}\geq(g^{f_{k^{*}}}_{j^{*}})^{2}

and thus for all
\eta\in(0,1)
,

q_{T^{M,G}}(\eta)\geq v_{-}q_{\chi_{1}^{2}}(\eta),

where
q_{\chi_{1}^{2}}
is the quantile function of a
\chi^{2}
random variable with one degree of freedom. Fix

s_{\gamma}:=v_{-}q_{\chi_{1}^{2}}((1-\gamma)/2).

(35)

By Lemma
35
,
F_{T^{M,G}}(\cdot)
is a continuous function and so

F_{T^{M,G}}(s_{\gamma})\leq(1-\gamma)/2,

(36)

which by the definition of the quantile function implies that

q_{T^{M,G}}((1-\gamma)/2)\geq s_{\gamma}.

(37)

If (A1)-(A3) holds, let
C=[80\big(1\vee C_{4,1}\vee C_{5,1}\vee C_{7,1}\vee C_{7,3}\big)]^{-1}
for the constants
C_{4,1}
and
C_{5,1}
,
C_{7,1}
and
C_{7,3}
introduced in the proof below; it can be seen in the proof that these constants depend only on the model parameters that appear in (A1)-(A3). If in addition (A4) holds let
\widetilde{C}=[50\big(C_{4,1}\vee C_{5,1}\vee C_{8,3}\vee C_{8,1}\vee 1\big)]^{-1}
, where
C_{8,1}
and
C_{8,3}
are likewise defined in the proof below and depend only on the model parameters of (A1)-(A4). Let
D_{\gamma}=[s_{\gamma}\wedge(1-\gamma)]/2
.

If
d_{n}:=d(n,K,T,N)
or
\widetilde{d}_{n}:=\widetilde{d}(n,K,T,N)
satisfy
d_{n}\geq CD_{\gamma},\widetilde{d}_{n}\geq\widetilde{C}D_{\gamma}
, then
the choice
C_{1-\gamma}=[D_{\gamma}C]^{-1}
implies that (
12
) holds under (A1)-(A3) and likewise for
\widetilde{C}_{1-\gamma}=[D_{\gamma}\widetilde{C}]^{-1}
if (A4) holds additionally. We therefore assume in the remainder of the proof that

d_{n}<CD_{\gamma}\mbox{ when working under (A1)-(A3) and }\widetilde{d}_{n}<\widetilde{C}D_{\gamma}\mbox{ under (A1)-(A4)}.

(38)

The remaining proof relies on many intermediate technical results which we will derive in the supplement below. Here, we provide the main steps and explain the high level ideas on the way. The following bound will be used repeatedly: for two generic random variables
X,Y
we have for any
\delta>0,s\in\mathbb{R}

\sup_{t\geq s}|{\mathrm{P}}(X\leq t)-{\mathrm{P}}(Y\leq t)|\leq{\mathrm{P}}(|X-Y|>\delta)+\sup_{t\geq s}{\mathrm{P}}(Y\in[t-\delta,t+\delta]).

(39)

It follows by elementary computations and the proof is omitted. A crucial role will be played by the following quantity related to anti-concentration of
T^{M,G}

\mathcal{A}(s,\delta):=\sup_{t\geq s}{\mathrm{P}}(|T^{M,G}-t|\leq\delta).

(40)

We first provide a general approach linking
{\mathrm{P}}(T^{d}\leq\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma})
to various approximation errors given next. We will give explicit bounds on those errors under the different sets of assumptions made in Theorem
1
at the end of the proof. Define

\displaystyle\Delta_{de,MG}(s;f)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{t\geq s}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}(T^{M,G}\leq t)-{\mathrm{P}}_{e}(T^{d,e}(f,e)\leq t)\Big|,

\displaystyle\Delta_{d,MG}(s)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{t\geq s}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}(T^{M,G}\leq t)-{\mathrm{P}}(T^{d}\leq t)\Big|,

\displaystyle\Delta_{M,MG}(s)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{t\geq s}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}(T^{M}\leq t)-{\mathrm{P}}(T^{M,G}\leq t)\Big|

\displaystyle\Delta_{Me,MG}(s;f)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{t\geq s}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}_{e}(T^{M,e}(\Phi,e)\leq t)-{\mathrm{P}}(T^{M,G}\leq t)\Big|.

We assume the existence of sequences
a_{n},b_{n},v_{n},w_{n}
with the property that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{P}}(\Delta_{de,MG}(f;s_{\gamma}/2)\geq a_{n})

\displaystyle\leq b_{n},

(41)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{P}}(\Delta_{Me,MG}(f;s_{\gamma}/2)\geq v_{n})

\displaystyle\leq w_{n}.

(42)

and also

a_{n}\leq\frac{d_{n}}{2C}

(43)

if (A1)-(A3) holds, and the same bound with
d_{n}
replaced by
\widetilde{d}_{n}
and
C
replaced by
\widetilde{C}
if in addition (A4) holds.
Later on we will show that such sequences exist under (A1)-(A3) and also (A1)-(A4) for sufficiently small value of
C
and
\widetilde{C}
; the sequences
a_{n}
and
v_{n}
will differ under the two different sets of conditions.

Over the course of the proof we will calculate various bounds in terms of the dimensional parameter
M
. We set
M=3\lceil n^{\frac{1}{12\alpha-2}}\rceil
and note that
3\leq M\leq 3(1+n^{\frac{1}{12\alpha-2}})\leq 6n^{\frac{1}{12\alpha-2}}
. However we use the dimensional parameter
M
as notation until the end of the proof when we confirm the rates in
n
,
T
,
K
, and
N
that are stated in Theorem
1
by plugging in the definition of
M
in terms of the above sequence in
n
. This facilitates readability and allows us to directly apply technical results proved in the supplement that apply to
L^{\infty}-L^{2}
mixed norms of random vectors defined on
(\mathbb{R}^{M})^{K}
.

Step 1:
\widehat{F}
and the conditional distribution of
T^{d,e}
given data are close

Recall that
e\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an isometric Gaussian independent of
(f,E)
. Define

\Delta_{1}(f,E):=\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\widehat{F}(t;f,E)-{\mathrm{P}}_{e}(T^{d,e}(f,e)\leq t)\Big|.

(44)

Let
\tau:=\sqrt{\frac{2\log(N)}{N}}
and define
A_{\tau}:=(\tau,1]
. Since
f
and
E
are independent and
\Delta_{1}(f,E)\leq 1
a.s the Fubini-Tonelli theorem gives, for
\mathbf{1}_{A_{\tau}}
denoting the indicator fo the set
A_{\tau}
,

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{A_{\tau}}\circ\Delta_{1}(f,E)]=\int_{((\mathbb{R}^{T})^{K})^{n}}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{A_{\tau}}\circ\Delta_{1}(x,E)]d\mu^{1}_{x},

(45)

where the real Borel probability measure
\mu^{1}
corresponds to the distribution of the collection
\{f_{k,i}(t_{\ell})-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(t_{\ell})\}_{k=1,i=1,\ell=1}^{K,n,T}
.

The Dvoretzky–Kiefer–Wolfowitz inequality (see
Massart 1990
) implies the inequality

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{A_{\tau}}\circ\Delta_{1}(x,E)]\leq 2\exp(-2N\tau^{2})\leq\frac{1}{N}

for every
x
since

\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{A_{\tau}}\circ\Delta_{1}(x,E)]={\mathrm{P}}\Big(\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}|\widehat{F}(t;x,E)-{\mathrm{P}}(T^{d,e}(x,e)\leq t)]|>\tau\Big)

and since, for fixed
x
,
\widehat{F}(t;x,E)
is the empirical cdf of
T^{d,e}(x,e^{1}),\dots,T^{d,e}(x,e^{N})
, an i.i.d. sample from the distribution of
T^{d,e}(x,e)
. Combined with (
45
) this shows

{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\Delta_{1}(f,E)>\sqrt{\frac{2\log(N)}{N}}\Big)\leq\frac{1}{N}

(46)

and completes Step 1.

Step 2: relating
\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}
and
q_{T^{M,G}}(\cdot)

Define

\kappa_{n}:=\sqrt{\frac{2\log(N)}{N}}+a_{n}

(47)

for
a_{n}
from (
41
) and also define the event

\Omega_{1}:=\Big\{\sup_{t\geq s_{\gamma}/2}\big|\widehat{F}(t)-F_{T^{M,G}}(t)\big|\leq\kappa_{n}\Big\}.

As seen in (
36
),
F_{T^{M,G}}(s_{\gamma}/2)\leq(1-\gamma)/2
. Also by (
43
) and the definition of
D_{\gamma}
, we have
\kappa_{n}\leq\sqrt{2}d_{n}+d_{n}/(2C)<(1-\gamma)/2
, and also
\kappa_{n}<s_{\gamma}/2
under the conditions (A1)-(A3), and we obtain the same inequalities if (A1)-(A4) hold. We apply Lemma
36
on the event
\Omega_{1}
with
\alpha=1-\gamma
,
G=F_{T^{M,G}}
,
F=\widehat{F}
,
\delta=\kappa_{n}
and
t_{*}=s_{\gamma}/2
. Since
G(t_{*})+\kappa_{n}<F_{T^{M,G}}(s_{\gamma}/2)+(1-\gamma)/2\leq(1-\gamma)=\alpha
we obtain that on
\Omega_{1}

q_{T^{M,G}}(1-\gamma-\kappa_{n})\leq\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}\leq q_{T^{M,G}}(1-\gamma+\kappa_{n}).

By a combination of (
46
) from Step 1 and the definition of
b_{n}
in (
41
) the union bound gives
{\mathrm{P}}(\Omega_{1}^{C})\leq b_{n}+N^{-1}
and thus

{\mathrm{P}}\Big(q_{T^{M,G}}(1-\gamma-\kappa_{n})\leq\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}\leq q_{T^{M,G}}(1-\gamma+\kappa_{n})\Big)\geq 1-b_{n}-\frac{1}{N}.

(48)

This completes Step 2.

Step 3: deriving a general bound on
|{\mathrm{P}}(T^{d}\leq\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma})-(1-\gamma)|

In the previous step we showed that
\kappa_{n}<(1-\gamma)/2
. Therefore by (
36
)

q_{T^{M,G}}(1-\gamma-\kappa_{n})\geq q_{T^{M,G}}((1-\gamma)/2)\geq s_{\gamma}.

(49)

Then, since on
\Omega_{1}
we have
\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}\leq q_{T^{M,G}}(1-\gamma+\kappa_{n})
and by (
49
)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{P}}(T^{d}\leq\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma})

\displaystyle={\mathrm{P}}(\{T^{d}\leq\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}\}\cap\Omega_{1})+{\mathrm{P}}(\{T^{d}\leq\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}\}\cap\Omega_{1}^{C})

\displaystyle\leq{\mathrm{P}}(T^{d}\leq q_{T^{M,G}}(1-\gamma+\kappa_{n}))+{\mathrm{P}}(\Omega_{1}^{C})

\displaystyle\leq{\mathrm{P}}(T^{M,G}\leq q_{T^{M,G}}(1-\gamma+\kappa_{n}))+\Delta_{d,MG}(s_{\gamma})+b_{n}+\frac{1}{N}

\displaystyle\leq 1-\gamma+\kappa_{n}+\Delta_{d,MG}(s_{\gamma})+b_{n}+\frac{1}{N},

where to get the last inequality we used absolute continuity of
T^{M,G}
, shown in Lemma
35
.
Similarly, since on
\Omega_{1}
we have
\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}\geq q_{T^{M,G}}(1-\gamma-\kappa_{n})\geq s_{\gamma}
by (
49
), it follows that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{P}}(T^{d}\leq\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma})

\displaystyle={\mathrm{P}}(\{T^{d}\leq\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}\}\cap\Omega_{1})+{\mathrm{P}}(\{T^{d}\leq\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}\}\cap\Omega_{1}^{C})

\displaystyle\geq{\mathrm{P}}(\{T^{d}\leq q_{T^{M,G}}(1-\gamma-\kappa_{n})\}\cap\Omega_{1})

\displaystyle={\mathrm{P}}(T^{d}\leq q_{T^{M,G}}(1-\gamma-\kappa_{n}))-{\mathrm{P}}(\{T^{d}\leq q_{T^{M,G}}(1-\gamma-\kappa_{n})\}\cap\Omega_{1}^{C})

\displaystyle\geq{\mathrm{P}}(T^{M,G}\leq q_{T^{M,G}}(1-\gamma-\kappa_{n}))-\Delta_{d,MG}(s_{\gamma})-{\mathrm{P}}(\Omega_{1}^{C})

\displaystyle\geq 1-\gamma-\kappa_{n}-\Delta_{d,MG}(s_{\gamma})-b_{n}-\frac{1}{N}.

where to get the last inequality we used absolute continuity of
T^{M,G}
. In summary, we find

\Big|{\mathrm{P}}(T^{d}\leq\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma})-(1-\gamma)\Big|\leq\kappa_{n}+\Delta_{d,MG}(s_{\gamma})+b_{n}+\frac{1}{N}.

(50)

Step 4: bounding
\Delta_{d,MG}
through
\Delta_{M,MG}

By Lemma
21
, there exists a constant
C_{4,1}\geq 1
(we choose this constant to be greater than one for technical reasons that will become apparent later) such that

\|T^{d}-T^{M}\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq C_{4,1}(M^{1-2\alpha}+T^{-q})[\log(K+1)]^{2}.

Let
\delta_{1,n}:=4C_{4,1}(M^{1-2\alpha}+T^{-q})[\log(K+1)]^{2}[\log(n)]^{2}\geq C_{4,1}(M^{1-2\alpha}+T^{-q})[\log(K+1)]^{2}[\log(2n)]^{2}
, so that by Lemma
41
(iv),

{\mathrm{P}}(|T^{d}-T^{M}|>\delta_{1,n})\leq\frac{1}{n}.

(51)

We verify in Remark
7
that under (A1)-(A3) or (A1)-(A4) we have
\delta_{1,n}\leq s_{\gamma}/2
, as the constants
C
and
\widetilde{C}
were chosen large enough (see (
38
)). Then by definition of
\Delta_{M,MG}
we have

\sup_{t\geq s_{\gamma}}{\mathrm{P}}(|T^{M}-t|\leq\delta_{1,n})\leq 2\Delta_{M,MG}(s_{\gamma}/2)+\mathcal{A}(s_{\gamma},\delta_{1,n}).

Combined with (
39
) and (
51
) this yields

\sup_{t\geq s_{\gamma}}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}(T^{d}\leq t)-{\mathrm{P}}(T^{M}\leq t)\Big|\leq 2\Delta_{M,MG}(s_{\gamma}/2)+\mathcal{A}(s_{\gamma},\delta_{1,n})+\frac{1}{n}.

The final bound of Step 4 is

\Delta_{d,MG}(s_{\gamma})=\sup_{t\geq s_{\gamma}}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}(T^{d}\leq t)-{\mathrm{P}}(T^{M,G}\leq t)\Big|\leq 3\Delta_{M,MG}(s_{\gamma}/2)+\mathcal{A}(s_{\gamma},\delta_{1,n})+\frac{1}{n},

(52)

where we have used that
\Delta_{M,MG}(\cdot)
is a non-increasing function.

Step 5: bounding
\Delta_{de,MG}
through
\Delta_{Me,MG}

Define

\Delta_{5}(f;s):=\sup_{t\geq s}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}_{e}(T^{M,e}(\Phi,e)\leq t)-{\mathrm{P}}_{e}(T^{d,e}(f,e)\leq t)\Big|.

Lemma
22
shows that there exists a random variable
W
such that almost surely

\|T^{M,e}(\Phi,e)-T^{d,e}(f,e)\|_{\Psi_{1}|\sigma(f)}\leq W

where
W
is a random variable with

\|W\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq C_{5,1}(M^{1-2\alpha}+T^{-q})[\log(K+1)]^{3},

and
C_{5,1}
is a constant that depends only on the fixed model parameters of (A1)-(A3). Thus by a conditional version of (
39
) we have a.s., for any
\delta_{n}<s_{\gamma}/2

\displaystyle\Delta_{5}(f;s_{\gamma})

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp(-\delta_{n}/W)+\sup_{t\geq s_{\gamma}}{\mathrm{P}}_{e}(|T^{M,e}(f,e)-t|\leq\delta_{n}).

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp(-\delta_{n}/W)+2\Delta_{Me,MG}(f;s_{\gamma}/2)+\mathcal{A}(s_{\gamma},\delta_{n}),

xwhere we have applied Lemma
41
(iv) conditionally, as demonstrated in Remark
8
. Let

\delta_{2,n}:=8(C_{5,1}\vee C_{4,1})(M^{1-2\alpha}+T^{-q})[\log(K+1)]^{3}[\log n]^{3}\geq\|W\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}[\log(2n)]^{3}.

We verify in Remark
7
that
\delta_{2,n}<s_{\gamma}/2
. The constant in the definition of
\delta_{2,n}
was chosen so that
\delta_{2,n}\geq\delta_{1,n}
. Then for
n\geq 2

{\mathrm{P}}(\exp(-\delta_{2,n}/W)\geq 1/n)={\mathrm{P}}(\delta_{2,n}/W\leq\log n)\leq{\mathrm{P}}(W/\|W\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\geq[\log 2n]^{2})\leq 1/n

where the last line follows from Lemma
41
(iv) since
\big\|W/\|W\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\big\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}=1
. Combining the bounds above we see that

{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\Delta_{de,MG}(f;s_{\gamma})>2/n+3\Delta_{Me,MG}(f;s_{\gamma}/2)+\mathcal{A}(s_{\gamma},\delta_{2,n})\Big)\leq\frac{1}{n}.

(53)

We used that under (A1)-(A3) or (A1)-(A4) we have
\delta_{2,n}\leq s_{\gamma}/2
, as the constants
C
and
\widetilde{C}
were chosen large enough (see (
38
)) which is verified in Remark
7
.
This completes Step 5.

Step 6: combining the bounds so far

Using the definition of
w_{n}
,
v_{n}
from (
42
) and the union bound we have

{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\Delta_{de,MG}(f;s_{\gamma})>2/n+3v_{n}+\mathcal{A}(s_{\gamma},\delta_{2,n})\Big)\leq\frac{1}{n}+w_{n}.

(54)

Equation (
54
) shows that we may set

b_{n}=1/n+w_{n},\hskip 14.22636pta_{n}=2/n+3v_{n}+\mathcal{A}(s_{\gamma},\delta_{2,n}).

(55)

Indeed, we show in Remark
7
that under (A1)-(A3) we have,
a_{n}\leq\frac{d_{n}}{2C}
and under (A1)-(A4) the same inequalities hold with
d_{n}
replaced by
\widetilde{d}_{n}
and
C
replaced by
\widetilde{C}
so that (
41
) and also (
43
) are satisfied.
Noting that
\mathcal{A}(s,\delta)
is non-decreasing in
\delta
we therefore have after
plugging in the values of
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
, by Equations (
50
), (
52
) (which controls the term
\Delta_{d,MG}(s_{\gamma})
that appears in (
50
)) we have by the union bound

\displaystyle\Big|{\mathrm{P}}(T^{d}\leq\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma})-(1-\gamma)\Big|\leq C_{6,1}\{v_{n}+\mathcal{A}(s_{\gamma},\delta_{2,n})+w_{n}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(N)}{N}}+\Delta_{M,MG}(s_{\gamma}/2)+\frac{1}{n}\},

(56)

for a constant
C_{6,1}
that depends only on the fixed model parameters of (A1)-(A3) if (A4) does not hold, and may depend additionally on (A4) if (A4) holds. We also note for use in a later proof that the arguments so far imply

{\mathrm{P}}\Big(q_{T^{M,G}}(1-\gamma-\theta_{n}-3v_{n})\leq\widehat{q}(1-\gamma)\leq q_{T^{M,G}}(1-\gamma+\theta_{n}+3v_{n})\Big)\geq 1-\frac{2}{n}-\frac{1}{N}-w_{n}

(57)

for

\theta_{n}:=\frac{2}{n}+A(s_{\gamma},\delta_{2,n})+\sqrt{\frac{2\log N}{N}}.

(58)

This bound is obtained from (
48
) and (
54
), the union bound, and plugging in the previously stated expressions for
\kappa_{n}
and
a_{n}
.

Step 7: finalizing the result under (A1)–(A3)

It remains to control all terms on the right-hand side of (
56
). By Lemma
4
, there is a constant
C_{7,1}
that depends only on fixed model parameters in (A1)-(A3) such that for all
n\geq 3
and all
K\geq 1
,

\mathcal{A}(s_{\gamma}/2,\delta_{2,n})\leq C_{7,1}(M^{1-2\alpha}+T^{-q})[\log(K+1)]^{3}[\log(n)]^{3}M\log(nK).

(59)

We set the free parameter
m
in the bound of Lemma
4
as
m=n
, and by our choice of
\delta_{1,n}
(and in particular our choice
C_{4,1}\geq 1
) it is easily seen that
\delta_{2,n}\geq\delta_{1,n}\geq\frac{1}{n}
. By Lemma
13
, for a constant
C_{7,2}
that depends only on fixed model parameters in (A1)-(A3) and all
n\geq 3
and all
K\geq 1
(recall that we previously set
M
as a function of
n
in such a way that
n\geq 3
implies
M\geq 3
),

\Delta_{M,MG}(s_{\gamma}/2)\leq C_{7,2}\Big(\frac{M^{10}(\log(Kn))^{10}}{n}\Big)^{1/6}.

By Lemma
15
for a constant
C_{7,3}
that depends only on fixed model parameters in (A1)-(A3) and all
n\geq 3
and all
K\geq 1
, (again we use
M\geq 3
),

{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\Delta_{Me,MG}(f;s_{\gamma}/2)>C_{7,3}\Big[M^{4/3}\log(Kn)\Big(\frac{\log(KMn)\log^{2}(n)}{n}\Big)^{1/6}\Big]\Big)\leq 1/n.

(60)

Thus we take
v_{n}=C_{7,3}\Big[M^{4/3}\log(Kn)\Big(\frac{\log(KMn)\log^{2}(n)}{n}\Big)^{1/6}\Big]
and
w_{n}=\frac{1}{n}
, and note that they satisfy (
42
). Recall that
M=3\lceil n^{\frac{1}{12\alpha-2}}\rceil
and so
M\leq 6n^{\frac{1}{12\alpha-2}}
. This upper bound on
M
balances the terms
[M^{10}n^{-1}]^{1/6}
and
M^{2-2\alpha}
and they are both of order
n^{\frac{1-\alpha}{6\alpha-1}}
. Noting that the leading
\log
factor is
[\log(K+1)]^{3}[\log(n)]^{3}\log(nK)
completes the proof under (A1)-(A3).

Step 8: finalizing the result under (A1)–(A4)

It remains to control all terms on the right-hand side of (
56
). By Lemma
5
, there is a constant
C_{8,1}
such that depends only on fixed model parameters in (A1)-(A4) such that for all
n\geq 3
and all
K\geq 1
, and all
M\geq 1
.

\mathcal{A}(s_{\gamma}/2,\delta_{2,n})\leq C_{8,1}(M^{1-2\alpha}+T^{-q})[\log(K+1)\log(n)]^{3}[\log(K+1)]^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{3\beta}{2\alpha-1}}[\log(n)]^{\frac{3\beta}{2\alpha-1}}.

(61)

We set the free parameter
m
in the bound of Lemma
5
as
m=n
, and by our choice of
\delta_{1,n}
(and in particular our choice
C_{4,1}\geq 1
) it is easily seen that
\delta_{2,n}\geq\delta_{1,n}\geq\frac{1}{n}
.
By Lemma
14
, for a constant
C_{8,2}
that depends only on fixed models parameters in (A1)-(A4), and all
n\geq 3
, and all
K\geq 1
, (recall that we defined
M
as a function of
M
so that
n\geq 3
satisfies
M\geq 3
),

\Delta_{M,MG}(s_{\gamma}/2)\leq C_{8,2}\Big[\frac{M^{4}[\log(nK)]^{4}}{n}\Big]^{1/6}\log^{1+\frac{3\beta}{2\alpha-1}}(K+1)\log^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{3\beta}{2\alpha-1}}(n).

By Lemma
16
, for a constant
C_{8,3}
that depends only on fixed model parameters, we may set

v_{n}=C_{8,3}\Big[M^{2/3}\log^{1/3}(Kn)\Big(\frac{\log(KMn)\log^{2}(n)}{n}\Big)^{1/6}\Big]\log^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{3\beta}{2\alpha-1}}(K+1)\log^{\frac{3\beta}{2\alpha-1}}(n)

and
w_{n}=\frac{1}{n}
. Following Step 7, let
M=3\lceil n^{\frac{1}{12\alpha-2}}\rceil
(it turns out the same choice of
M
works in both cases), and note that
M\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{12\alpha-2}}
. This choice balances the terms
[M^{4}n^{-1}]^{1/6}
and
M^{1-2\alpha}
and they are both of order
n^{\frac{\frac{1}{2}-\alpha}{6\alpha-1}}
. Noting that the leading
\log
factor is
[\log(K+1)]^{\frac{7}{2}+\frac{3\beta}{2\alpha-1}}[\log n]^{3+\frac{3\beta}{2\alpha-1}}
completes the proof.

\Box

S.5.2
Proof of Theorem
2

Fix
\gamma\in(0,1)
and fix
\varepsilon\in(0,\gamma)
. Recall the constants
C
and
\widetilde{C}
and
D_{\gamma}
defined in the proof of Theorem
1
above (
38
). Let
D_{\gamma,\varepsilon}=D_{\gamma}\wedge\varepsilon
, and assume without loss of generality as in (
38
) (but this time with
D_{\gamma}
replaced by
D_{\gamma,\varepsilon}
) that

\displaystyle d_{n}

\displaystyle<\big(C\wedge C_{3,2}\wedge C_{4,4}\big)D_{\gamma,\varepsilon}\mbox{ when working under (A1)-(A3) and }

(62)

\displaystyle\widetilde{d}_{n}

\displaystyle<\big(\widetilde{C}\wedge C_{3,3}\wedge C_{4,4}\big)D_{\gamma,\varepsilon}\mbox{ under (A1)-(A4)}.

(63)

where the constants
C_{4,4},C_{3,2}
and
C_{3,3}
are defined in the proof below. For
1\leq k\leq K
, let

\displaystyle v^{k}:=\sqrt{\frac{1}{T}\sum_{i=1}^{T}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{f}^{k}_{i}(t_{\ell})}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}},\hskip 14.22636pt\mu^{k}:=\sqrt{\frac{1}{T}\sum_{i=1}^{T}\Big(\mu_{k}(t_{\ell})\Big)^{2}}.

Recall the definition of
s_{\gamma}
in (
35
). Set
B_{1}:=\{(s_{\gamma}\vee 1)[\log(2)]^{-1}\}\vee\{D_{1}^{2}\}
for
D_{1}
defined later in the proof. If
\sqrt{n}\mu^{k}-\sqrt{q_{G}(1-\gamma+\varepsilon)}\leq\sqrt{s_{\gamma}}

the right-hand side of (
22
) is non-positive for
B=B_{1}
, and the inequality holds trivially. From now on we assume that

\sqrt{n}\mu^{k}-\sqrt{q_{G}(1-\gamma+\varepsilon)}>\sqrt{s_{\gamma}}.

By the reverse triangle inequality for the Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{T}
,

\sqrt{T_{n}^{k}}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{T}\sum_{i=1}^{T}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{f}^{k}_{i}(t_{\ell})+\mu_{k}(t_{\ell})}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}}\geq\Bigg|\sqrt{\frac{1}{T}\sum_{i=1}^{T}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{f}^{k}_{i}(t_{\ell})}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}}-\sqrt{\frac{1}{T}\sum_{i=1}^{T}\Big(\sqrt{n}\mu_{k}(t_{\ell})\Big)^{2}}\Bigg|,

which implies
\sqrt{T_{n}^{k}}\geq\sqrt{n}\mu^{k}-v^{k}
and so

{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\sqrt{T_{n}^{k}}\geq\sqrt{\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}}\Big)\geq{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\sqrt{n}\mu^{k}\geq v^{k}+\sqrt{\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}}\Big).

(64)

In the remainder of the proof we give a lower bound to the right hand side of (
64
).

Step 1: relating
\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}
and
q_{T^{G}}(\cdot)

By Lemma
28
, there exists a
K
dimensional Gaussian process
\widetilde{G}=\widetilde{G}_{n}
on
(C[I])^{K}
(to lighten notation we suppress dependence of
\widetilde{G}
on
n
). With the property that for all
1\leq k_{1}\leq k_{2}\leq K
, and all
t_{1},t_{2}\in I
and all
n
,

\Cov(\widetilde{G}^{k_{1}}(t_{1}),\widetilde{G}^{k_{2}}(t_{2}))=\Cov\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{f}_{i}^{k_{1}}}{\sqrt{n}}(t_{1}),\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{f}_{i}^{k_{2}}}{\sqrt{n}}(t_{2})\Big).

We now show that that the process
\widetilde{G}
satisfies (A1) and (A2) (with the constants
C_{H}
and
C_{\phi}
possibly enlarged by a numeric multiplicative factor) and (A3), (A4) whenever
\widetilde{f}
does.

With respect to (A1), denoting by
\widetilde{G}^{k}
the
k^{th}
marginal process of
\widetilde{G}
, we have that
for all
1\leq k\leq K
, and all
t_{1},t_{2}\in I
,

\displaystyle\|{\widetilde{G}^{k}}(t_{1})-{\widetilde{G}^{k}}(t_{2})\|_{\Psi_{1}}=\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}(\Var({\widetilde{G}^{k}}(t_{1})-{\widetilde{G}^{k}}(t_{2})))^{1/2}=\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}(\Var(\widetilde{f}{{}^{k}}(t_{1})-\widetilde{f}{{}^{k}}(t_{2})))^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}C_{2,1}\|\widetilde{f}{{}^{k}}(t_{1})-\widetilde{f}{{}^{k}}(t_{2})\|_{\Psi_{1}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}C_{2,1}C_{H}|s-t|^{q},

where in the first equality we used that the increments of each
G_{k}
are Gaussian and
\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}
is equal to the
\Psi_{1}
norm of a standard Gaussian and in the first inequality we utilize Lemma
42
and the constant
C_{2,1}
defined in its statement, and in the final inequality we use assumption (A1). Therefore
\widetilde{G}
satisfies (A1) (with constant
\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}C_{2,1}C_{H}
instead of
C_{H}
)

With respect to (A2), by Lemma
28
, and using the notation introduced in there, the covariance function of the KL scores of
\widetilde{G}
equals the covariance function of the KL scores of
\widetilde{f}
so that
\widetilde{G}
also satisfies (A2):

\|\zeta_{i}^{k}\|_{\psi_{1}}=\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}\sqrt{\Var(\zeta_{i}^{k})}=\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}\sqrt{\Var(\phi_{i}^{f_{k}})}\leq\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}C_{2,1}\|\phi_{i}^{f_{k}}\|_{\psi_{1}}

where we use Lemma
42
and the constant
C_{2,1}
defined in its statement in the last inequality. Therefore
\widetilde{G}
satisfies (A2) (with constant
\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}C_{2,1}C_{\phi}
instead of
C_{\phi}
) whenever
\widetilde{f}
does and also (A3), (A4) whenever
\widetilde{f}
does (with the same constant), because their Karhunen–Loève scores have the same second order properties. Define for each
1\leq k\leq K

\displaystyle T^{\widetilde{G},k}(\widetilde{G}_{1}^{k},\dots,\widetilde{G}_{n}^{k})=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widetilde{G}^{k}_{i}(t_{\ell})\Big)^{2}

(65)

\displaystyle T^{\widetilde{G}}(\widetilde{G}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{G}_{n}):=\max_{k=1}^{K}T^{\widetilde{G},k},

(66)

\displaystyle T^{M,\widetilde{G}}(\widetilde{G}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{G}_{n}):=\max_{k=1}^{K}\Big\|\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\oplus_{j=1}^{M}\zeta^{k}_{i}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big\|^{2}_{2},

(67)

where
\widetilde{G}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{G}_{n}
are i.i.d copies of
\widetilde{G}
. Noting the definitions of
T^{d}
and
T^{M}
in (
27
), (
28
) it is seen that
T^{\widetilde{G}}
and
T^{M,\widetilde{G}}
are derived from
\widetilde{G}
in the same way that
T^{d}
and
T^{M}
are derived from
\widetilde{f}
, and we previously showed that
\widetilde{G}
satisfies (A1) and (A2). Therefore we may apply Lemma
21
with
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
replaced by
\widetilde{G}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{G}_{n}
to obtain

\|T^{\widetilde{G}}-T^{M,\widetilde{G}}\|_{\psi_{1/2}}\leq C_{3,1}[\log(K+1)]^{2}(M^{1-2\alpha}+T^{-q})

for a constant
C_{3,1}\geq 1
that depends only on the fixed model parameters in (A1)-(A3). Let
\delta_{3,n}=4C_{3,1}(M^{1-2\alpha}+T^{-q})[\log(K+1)]^{2}[\log(n)]^{2}\geq C_{3,1}(M^{1-2\alpha}+T^{-q})[\log(K+1)]^{2}[\log(2n)]^{2}
and set
M=3\lceil n^{\frac{1}{12\alpha-2}}\rceil
, as in the proof of Theorem
1
. By (
39
) and the definition of
\mathcal{A}(\cdot)
we obtain for any
s>0

\sup_{t\geq s}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}(T^{\widetilde{G}}\leq t)-{\mathrm{P}}(T^{M,\widetilde{G}}\leq t)\Big|\leq{\mathrm{P}}(|T^{M,\widetilde{G}}-T^{\widetilde{G}}|\geq\delta_{3,n})+\mathcal{A}(s,\delta_{3,n})\leq\frac{1}{n}+\mathcal{A}(s,\delta_{3,n}),

(68)

where we applied Lemma
41
(iv) to bound
{\mathrm{P}}(|T^{M,\widetilde{G}}-T^{\widetilde{G}}|\geq\delta_{3,n})
in the last line. We also used that
T^{M,\widetilde{G}}\overset{d}{=}T^{M,G}
. This is true since
n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}\widetilde{G}_{i}
has the same covariance structure as
n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}\widetilde{f}_{i}
and their KL scores, which are determined by only by second order information, have the same covariance structure. Moreover, they are both multivariate Gaussian (see Lemma
28
) and centered, thus they have the same law. Likewise, we have
T^{\widetilde{G}}\overset{d}{=}T^{G}
, which follows from the definition of
T^{G}
in the statement of the Lemma and the fact that the Gaussian vectors described there is equal in distribution to the process
\widetilde{G}
after it has been evaluated on the points
\{t_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{T}
. Recall the sequences
\theta_{n}
and
v_{n}
defined in the proof of Theorem
1
. Note that

q_{T^{M,G}}(1-\gamma+\theta_{n}+3v_{n})\geq q_{T^{M,G}}((1-\gamma)/2)\geq s_{\gamma}>s_{\gamma}/2.

The first inequality follows from positivity of
\theta_{n}
and
v_{n}
and the second from (
37
). It follows from (
68
) with
s=s_{\gamma}/2
and Lemma
36
(with
t_{*}=s_{\gamma}/2
,
F
the cumulative distribution function of
T^{M,G}
,
G
the cumulative distribution function of
T^{G}
,
\delta=\frac{1}{n}+\mathcal{A}(s_{\gamma}/2,\delta_{3,n})
,
\alpha=1-\gamma+\theta_{n}+3v_{n}
) that

q_{T^{G}}\big(1-\gamma+\theta_{n}+3v_{n}+n^{-1}+\mathcal{A}(s_{\gamma}/2,\delta_{3,n})\big)\geq q_{T^{M,G}}\big(1-\gamma+\theta_{n}+3v_{n}\big).

It then follows from (
57
) that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}\leq q_{T^{G}}\big(1-\gamma+\theta_{n}+3v_{n}+n^{-1}+\mathcal{A}(s_{\gamma}/2,\delta_{3,n})\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}\leq q_{T^{M,G}}\big(1-\gamma+\theta_{n}+3v_{n}\big)\Big)\geq 1-\frac{2}{n}-\frac{1}{N}-w_{n}

We note that in Steps 7 and 8 of the proof of Theorem
1
,
w_{n}
is set as
w_{n}=\frac{1}{n}
.
We also note that there there is a constant
C_{\delta,3,1}\geq 1
such that
\delta_{3,n}\leq C_{\delta,3,1}\delta_{1,n}
where
\delta_{1,n}
is defined in the proof of Theorem
1
. Under (A1)-(A3) this implies that there is a constant
C_{3,2}
such that

\mathcal{A}(s_{\gamma}/2,\delta_{3,n})\leq C_{3,2}(M^{1-2\alpha}+T^{-q})[\log(K+1)]^{3}[\log(n)]^{3}M\log(nK).

(69)

where we have argued as we did to obtain (
59
) and used that (when applying Lemma
4
)
\delta_{3,n}\geq\frac{1}{n}
. Likewise under (A1)-(A4),

\mathcal{A}(s_{\gamma}/2,\delta_{3,n})\leq C_{3,3}(M^{1-2\alpha}+T^{-q})[\log(K+1)\log(n)]^{3}[\log(K+1)]^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{3\beta}{2\alpha-1}}[\log(n)]^{\frac{3\beta}{2\alpha-1}}

(70)

where we argue as we did leading up to (
61
).

We further note that
\theta_{n}+3v_{n}+\frac{1}{n}+\mathcal{A}(s_{\gamma}/2,\delta_{3,n})\leq\varepsilon
under both sets of assumptions that we consider, as shown in Remark
7
. Therefore,

{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}\leq q_{T^{G}}\big(1-\gamma+\varepsilon\big)\Big)\geq 1-\frac{3}{n}-\frac{1}{N}.

(71)

Step 2: completion of Proof

Combining (
64
) and (
71
) we have

\displaystyle{\mathrm{P}}\Big(T^{k}_{n}\geq\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}\Big)={\mathrm{P}}\Big(\sqrt{T^{k}_{n}}\geq\sqrt{\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}}\Big)\geq{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\sqrt{n}\mu^{k}\geq v^{k}+\sqrt{\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\sqrt{n}\mu^{k}\geq v^{k}+\sqrt{q_{T^{G}}\big(1-\gamma+\varepsilon)\big)}\Big)-\frac{3}{n}-\frac{1}{N}

(72)

We now make the following definitions for all
1\leq k\leq K
:

\displaystyle T^{M,k}(\Phi_{1,k},\dots,\Phi_{n,k}):=\Big\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Phi_{i,k}\Big\|_{2}^{2},

(73)

\displaystyle T^{M,G,k}(g):=\|\pi_{k}g\|_{2}^{2},

(74)

\displaystyle\Delta_{M,MG,k}(s):=\sup_{t\geq s}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}(T^{M,k}\leq t)-{\mathrm{P}}(T^{M,G,k}\leq t)\Big|,

(75)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{k}(s,\delta):=\sup_{t\geq s}{\mathrm{P}}(|T^{M,G,k}-t|\leq\delta).

(76)

We note that
T^{M,k}
is a version of
T^{M}
(compare (
28
)) where we only consider one dimension, and similar comments link
T^{M,G,k}
to
T^{M,G}
(c.f. (
29
)) and
[v^{k}]^{2}
to
T^{d}
; see (
27
). Thus we may argue as was done in Step 4 of the proof of Theorem
1
to arrive at (
52
) and obtain

\sup_{t\geq s_{\gamma}}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}([v^{k}]^{2}\leq t)-{\mathrm{P}}(T^{G,M,k}\leq t)\Big|\leq 3\Delta_{M,MG,k}(s_{\gamma}/2)+\mathcal{A}_{k}(s_{\gamma},\delta_{4,n})+\frac{1}{n}

where
\delta_{4,n}:=C_{4,4}(M^{1-2\alpha}+T^{-q})[\log(n)]^{2}
. We show in Remark
7
that
\delta_{4,n}<s_{\gamma}/2
. Similarly,

\sup_{t\geq s_{\gamma}}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}(T^{G,k}\leq t)-{\mathrm{P}}(T^{G,M,k}\leq t)\Big|\leq 3\Delta_{M,MG,k}(s_{\gamma}/2)+\mathcal{A}_{k}(s_{\gamma},\delta_{4,n})+\frac{1}{n}

where we have replaced
\widetilde{f}_{1}^{k},\dots,\widetilde{f}_{n}^{k}
with
\widetilde{G}_{1}^{k},\dots,\widetilde{G}_{n}^{k}
similarly to the argument in Step 1 of the present proof. Combining the results above, we find

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq s_{\gamma}}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}([v^{k}]^{2}\leq t)-{\mathrm{P}}(T^{G,k}\leq t)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\geq s_{\gamma}}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}([v^{k}]^{2}\leq t)-{\mathrm{P}}(T^{G,M,k}\leq t)\Big|+\sup_{t\geq s_{\gamma}}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}(T^{G,k}\leq t)-{\mathrm{P}}(T^{G,M,k}\leq t)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq 2\Big(3\Delta_{M,MG,k}(s_{\gamma}/2)+\mathcal{A}_{k}(s_{\gamma},\delta_{4,n})+\frac{1}{n}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{5,1}d_{n},

where
C_{5,1}
is a sufficiently large constant that depends only on the model parameters in (A1)-(A3). If instead (A1)-(A4) holds, a similar bound with
d_{n}
replaced by
\widetilde{d}_{n}
and
C_{5,1}
replaced by a possibly different constant
C_{5,1,1}
that depends on the model parameters of (A1)-(A4) holds. Here, the last inequality follows from a direct computation after noting that
\mathcal{A}_{k}
is a one-dimensional version of
\mathcal{A}
and can be bounded by the same upper bounds as in (
59
) under conditions (A1)-(A3) and (
61
) under (A1)-(A4).

Finally, recall that we assumed in the beginning of the proof that
\sqrt{n}\mu^{k}-\sqrt{q_{T^{G}}\big(1-\gamma+\varepsilon)}\geq\sqrt{s_{\gamma}}>0
and thus

\displaystyle{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\sqrt{n}\mu^{k}\geq v^{k}+\sqrt{q_{T^{G}}\big(1-\gamma+\varepsilon)}\Big)={\mathrm{P}}\Big(v^{k}\leq\sqrt{n}\mu^{k}-\sqrt{q_{T^{G}}\big(1-\gamma+\varepsilon)}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathrm{P}}\Big([v^{k}]^{2}\leq\big(\sqrt{n}\mu^{k}-\sqrt{q_{T^{G}}\big(1-\gamma+\varepsilon)}\big)_{+}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathrm{P}}\Big(T^{G,k}\leq\big(\sqrt{n}\mu^{k}-\sqrt{q_{T^{G}}\big(1-\gamma+\varepsilon)}\big)_{+}^{2}\Big)-C_{5,1}d_{n}.

Combined with (
72
) we obtain under (A1)-(A3)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{P}}\Big(T^{k}_{n}\geq\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathrm{P}}\Big(T^{G,k}\leq\big(\sqrt{n}\mu^{k}-\sqrt{q_{T^{G}}\big(1-\gamma+\varepsilon)}\big)_{+}^{2}\Big)-C_{5,1}d_{n}-\frac{3}{n}-\frac{1}{N}

\displaystyle\geq{\mathrm{P}}\Big(T^{G,k}\leq\big(\sqrt{n}\mu^{k}-\sqrt{q_{T^{G}}\big(1-\gamma+\varepsilon)}\big)_{+}^{2}\Big)-C_{6,1}d_{n}.

(77)

Under (A1)-(A4), a similar bound holds with
d_{n}
replaced by
\widetilde{d}_{n}
and
C_{6,1}
replaced by a possibly different constant
\widetilde{C}_{6,1}
.

To give a further lower bound, it is now useful to develop a tail bound for
\sqrt{T^{G,k}}
. Note that

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{T^{G,k}}\|_{\Psi_{2}}=\|T^{G,k}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\Psi_{1}}=\Big(\Big\|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}[G_{n}^{k,\ell}]^{2}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\Big(\frac{1}{T}\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\|[G_{n}^{k,\ell}]^{2}\|_{\Psi_{1}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\Big(\frac{1}{T}\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\|G_{n}^{k,\ell}\|_{\Psi_{2}}^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\big(\max_{\ell=1}^{T}\|G_{n}^{k,\ell}\|_{\Psi_{2}}^{2}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\max_{\ell=1}^{T}\|G_{n}^{k,\ell}\|_{\Psi_{2}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}\max_{\ell=1}^{T}\Var(f_{k,1}(t_{\ell}))}\leq D_{1},

for a constant
D_{1}
that depends only on
\alpha
,
C_{\phi}
,
C_{H}
and
q
under either set of conditions we consider. We have applied Lemma
42
in the first equality, the triangle inequality for the
\psi_{1}
norm in the first inequality, Lemma
42
in the third and fourth equality. The penultimate inequality uses the fact that if
Z
is centered Gaussian,
\|Z\|_{\Psi_{2}}=\sqrt{\tfrac{8}{3}\Var(Z)}
, and the fact that
G_{n}
defined in the statement of the Lemma we are proving satisfies
\Var(G_{n}^{k,\ell})=\Var(f_{k,1}(t_{\ell}))
for all
1\leq k\leq K
, and all
1\leq\ell\leq T
. The final inequality uses Lemma
33
(note that we do not explicitly assume that
\sup_{t}\Var(f_{k,1}(t))
is finite in Assumptions
1
) and the fact that
f_{k,1}
is centered which implies
\sqrt{\Var(f_{k,1}(t_{\ell}))}=\mathbb{E}[(f_{k,1}(t_{\ell}))^{2}]^{1/2}\leq\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t}(f_{k,1}(t))^{2}]^{1/2}\leq C_{1,2}\big\|\sup_{t}|f_{k,1}(t)|\big\|_{\psi_{1}}
where the last inequality utilizes Lemma
42
. It follows then from Lemma (
41
)(iv) combined with (
77
) that

{\mathrm{P}}\Big(T^{k}_{n}\geq\widehat{q}_{1-\gamma}\Big)\geq 1-2\exp\big(-(\sqrt{n}\mu^{k}-\sqrt{q_{T^{G}}(1-\gamma+\varepsilon)})^{2}_{+}/D_{1}^{2}\big)-C_{6,1}d_{n}

under (A1)-(A3) and similarly with
\widetilde{d}_{n}
in place of
d_{n}
and a possibly different constant
\widetilde{C}_{6,1}
under (A1)-(A4). This completes the proof.

\Box

.

) and (

C=[80\big(C_{4,1}\vee C_{5,1}\vee C_{7,3}\vee C_{7,1}\vee 1\big)]^{-1},\hskip 14.22636pt\widetilde{C}=[50\big(C_{4,1}\vee C_{5,1}\vee C_{8,3}\vee C_{8,1}\vee 1\big)]^{-1}

and we have assumed without loss of generality that

In many cases we use a calculator to determine the size of numeric constants.

It holds that
\delta_{4,n}<s_{\gamma}/2
under (A1)-(A3) and also under (A1)-(A4)

Noting that by similar calculations as performed for
\delta_{2,n}
above we have under (A1)-(A3) noting that
6/(\log(6)\times(\log 3)^{4})\leq 3
,

\delta_{4,n}:=C_{4,4}(M^{1-2\alpha}+T^{-q})[\log(n)]^{2}\leq 3(C_{5,1}\vee C_{4,1}\vee C_{4,4})d_{n}<s_{\gamma}/2.

Under (A1)-(A4) we have similarly

\delta_{4,n}:=C_{4,4}(M^{1-2\alpha}+T^{-q})[\log(n)]^{2}\leq 6(C_{5,1}\vee C_{4,1}\vee C_{4,4})\widetilde{d}_{n}<s_{\gamma}/2.

S.6
Technical results

S.6.1
Approximations of Convex Sets by Polytopes

Let
\bar{B}^{M}_{t}\subset\mathbb{R}^{M}
be the closed unit ball of radius
t
for
\|\cdot\|_{2}
, and let
S^{M-1}_{t}=\partial\bar{B}^{M}_{t}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{M},\|x\|_{2}=t\}
. For
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
let
A\times B\subset\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
be the cartesian product of
A
and
B
. Sometimes we will write
(\mathbb{R}^{M})^{K}
instead of
\mathbb{R}^{MK}
to emphasize the identity
\mathbb{R}^{MK}=\times_{k=1}^{K}\mathbb{R}^{M}=\oplus_{k=1}^{K}\mathbb{R}^{M}
. Sometimes we will drop superscripts and subscripts to lighten notation where no confusion will arise.

Given
x\in(\mathbb{R}^{M})^{K}
we approximate each level set (in a sense defined below) of the Euclidean
L^{\infty}-L^{2}
mixed norm
x\rightarrow\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}x\|_{2}
(recall the definition of
\pi_{k}
in
S.4
) by a level set of a polytope (defined below) that has special properties.

Definition 1
.

Let

E_{t}:=\Big\{x\in(\mathbb{R}^{M})^{K}:\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}(x)\|_{2}\leq t\Big\}=\times_{k=1}^{K}\bar{B}^{M}_{t}.

Our general strategy for working with a set
E_{t}
is to approximate it by half-spaces. Given a finite collection of unit vectors
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a positive real number
s
, we define

A(\mathcal{V},s)=\{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\forall v\in\mathcal{V},v^{\top}w\leq s\}.

We will refer to sets
A(\mathcal{V},s)
as polytopes. Note that for any
\mathcal{V}
,
\bar{B}^{d}_{s}\subset A(\mathcal{V},s)
.

Lemma 1
.

For each pair of real numbers
t
and
\varepsilon
satisfying
t\geq\varepsilon>0
, and each
K\geq 1
,
M\geq 3
, there exists a collection of unit vectors
\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}
such that

1.

|\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}|\leq K(1+t\varepsilon^{-1})^{\frac{M-1}{2}}4^{M-1}
so that
\log(|\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}|)\leq 3M\log(K(1+t\varepsilon^{-1}))
.

2.

E_{t-\varepsilon}\subset A(\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t},t-\varepsilon)\subset E_{t}\subset A(\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t},t)\subset E_{t+\varepsilon}

3.

If
v\in\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}
there is a unique
k
such that
\pi_{k}v\neq 0
.

Definition 2
.

For
t\geq\varepsilon
,
K\geq 1
,
M\geq 3
let
\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{M})^{K}

5
5

5

Note that applying a fixed non-identity orthogonal transformation of the form
O=\oplus^{K}_{k=1}O_{k}
,
O_{k}\in O(M,\mathbb{R})
, to each element of a collection
\mathcal{V}^{1}_{\varepsilon,t}
of Lemma
1
produces a distinct collection
\mathcal{V}^{2}_{\varepsilon,t}
satisfying the same properties.
be a collection of unit vectors satisfying conditions (a)–(c)of Lemma
1
.

To prove Lemma
1
we use Lemma
3
whose proof relies on the estimate from Lemma
2
. Lemma
2
is a restatement of Lemma 2.3 on page 11 of
Ball et al. 1997
. Lemma
3
is a straightforward generalization of an argument contained in
Ball et al. 1997
.

Lemma 2
(Lower bound on surface area of spherical cap).

Let
S
be the boundary of the
M
dimensional unit ball
B
. Let
\mu
be normalized surface measure on
S
, so that
\mu(S)=1
. Let
A_{r,v}=\{x\in S,\|x-v\|_{2}\leq r\}
. Then for
0\leq r\leq 2
,
\mu(A_{r,v})\geq\frac{1}{2}(r/2)^{M-1}
.

Lemma 3
(Approximation of Ball by Polytopes).

Let
B_{t}\subset\mathbb{R}^{M}
be the closed unit ball of radius
t
. Suppose
M\geq 3
. For each
1\geq\varepsilon>0
and every
t\geq\varepsilon
there exists a collection of unit vectors
\mathcal{V}
such that

1.

|\mathcal{V}|\leq(1+\varepsilon^{-1})^{\frac{M-1}{2}}4^{M-1}

2.

A(\mathcal{V},\frac{t}{1+\epsilon})\subset B_{t}\subset A(\mathcal{V},t)

S.6.2
Proofs for Section
S.6.1

Proof of Lemma
1

Fix
t
and
\varepsilon
satisfying
t\geq\varepsilon>0
. We first prove the claim in case
K=1
. By Lemma
3
there exists a collection
\mathcal{V}
such that

A(\mathcal{V},\tfrac{t}{1+t^{-1}\varepsilon})\subset B_{t}\subset A(\mathcal{V},t)\subset(1+\tfrac{\varepsilon}{t})B_{t}=B_{t+\varepsilon},

(79)

and
|\mathcal{V}|\leq(1+t\varepsilon^{-1})^{\frac{M-1}{2}}4^{M-1}
. Since

\frac{t}{1+t^{-1}\varepsilon}=\frac{t^{2}}{t+\varepsilon}>\frac{t^{2}}{t+\varepsilon}-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{t+\varepsilon}=t-\varepsilon,

we have

A(\mathcal{V},t-\varepsilon)\subset A(\mathcal{V},\tfrac{t}{1+t^{-1}\varepsilon}),

and so,

A(\mathcal{V},t-\varepsilon)\subset B_{t}.

The inclusions
B_{t-\varepsilon}\subset A(\mathcal{V},t-\varepsilon)
and
B_{t}\subset A(\mathcal{V},t)
hold trivially by the remark following Definition
1
. Note that

\begin{split}&\log\Big(|\mathcal{V}|\Big)\leq 2(M-1)\log(2)+\Big((M-1)/2\Big)\log(1+t\varepsilon^{-1})\\
&\leq 2(M-1)\log(1+t\varepsilon^{-1})+\Big((M-1)/2\Big)\log(1+t\varepsilon^{-1})\\
&\leq 3M\log(1+t\varepsilon^{-1}).\\
\end{split}

(80)

This proves the claim in case
K=1
and we now prove the general case. To each
v\in\mathcal{V}\subset\mathbb{R}^{M}
we now assign a collection of
K
vectors
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{M})^{K}
. Let
v_{1}(v)=v\oplus_{i=2}^{K}\mathbf{0}_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}
, let
v_{K}(v)=\oplus_{i=1}^{K-1}\mathbf{0}_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\oplus v
, and for
2\leq k\leq K-1
, let
v_{k}(v)=\oplus_{i=1}^{k-1}\mathbf{0}_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}\oplus v\oplus_{i=k+1}^{K}\mathbf{0}_{\mathbb{R}^{M}}
. Now let
\mathcal{V}_{K}=\cup_{i=1}^{K}\cup_{v\in\mathcal{V}}\{v_{k}(v)\}
. It is straightforward to see that

E_{t-\varepsilon}\subset A(\mathcal{V}_{K},t-\varepsilon)\subset E_{t}\subset A(\mathcal{V}_{K},t)\subset E_{t+\varepsilon},

and that if
w\in\mathcal{V_{K}}
,
\pi_{k}w\neq 0
for a unique
k
. Then for every
K\geq 1

\displaystyle\log\Big(|\mathcal{V}_{K}|\Big)=\log\Big(K|\mathcal{V}|\Big)=\log\Big(K\Big)+\log\Big(|\mathcal{V}|\Big)\leq 3M\log\Big(K\Big)+3M\log\Big(1+t\varepsilon^{-1}\Big)

\displaystyle=3M\log\Big(K(1+t\varepsilon^{-1})\Big),

where in the first inequality we used the bound of (
80
).

\Box

Proof of Lemma
3

It suffices to find a collection
\mathcal{V}
of asserted size such that

B_{1}\subset A(\mathcal{V},1)\subset(1+\varepsilon)B_{1}.

Scaling by
\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon}
all sets in the chain of inclusions then gives

A(\mathcal{V},\tfrac{1}{1+\epsilon})\subset B_{1}\subset A(\mathcal{V},1).

Scaling next by
t
gives

A(\mathcal{V},\tfrac{t}{1+\epsilon})\subset B_{t}\subset A(\mathcal{V},t).

The remainder of the proof is a modification of the argument presented on page 11 of
Ball et al. 1997
, which deals with the case
\varepsilon=1
. We modify it to cover the case of arbitrary
1\geq\varepsilon>0
and add some details.

We will show that
\mathcal{V}
can be chosen so that
|\mathcal{V}|\leq(1+\varepsilon^{-1})^{\frac{M-1}{2}}4^{M-1}
while

B_{1}\subset A(\mathcal{V},1)\subset(1+\varepsilon)B_{1}.

Recalling that
\mathcal{V}
is a collection of unit vectors
B_{1}\subset A(\mathcal{V},1)
is automatic. The inclusion
A(\mathcal{V},1)\subset(1+\varepsilon)B_{1}
holds if and only if
\forall\theta\in S_{1}
,
\exists v\in\mathcal{V}
such that
\langle\theta,v\rangle\geq\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon}
, however we only require the ‘if’ direction of this statement, which is straightforward to check using the definitions of
A(\mathcal{V},1)
and
(1+\varepsilon)B_{1}=B_{1+\varepsilon}
.

We will now identify a collection
\mathcal{V}
with property that
\forall\theta\in S_{1}
,
\exists v\in\mathcal{V}
such that
\langle\theta,v\rangle\geq\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon}
. To this end, let
u
be a generic unit vector, and consider the set

C(\varepsilon,u)=\{y\in S_{1},\langle u,y\rangle\geq\tfrac{1}{1+\varepsilon}\}.

Elementary computations shows that for
\widetilde{r}=\sqrt{\frac{2\varepsilon}{1+\varepsilon}}
.

C(\varepsilon,u)=\{y\in S_{1},\|y-u\|_{2}\leq\widetilde{r}\}.

Let
\{p_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
be a maximal
\widetilde{r}
packing of
S_{1}
with respect to the Euclidean metric inherited from
\mathbb{R}^{M}
. By the triangle inequality, the sets
\{y\in S_{1},\|y-p_{i}\|_{2}\leq\frac{\widetilde{r}}{2}\}
are disjoint. Let
\mu
be normalized surface measure, and note that
0\leq\widetilde{r}\leq 2
. By Lemma
2
,

m\frac{1}{2}(\widetilde{r}/4)^{M-1}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu(\{y\in S_{1},\|y-p_{i}\|_{2}\leq\frac{\widetilde{r}}{2}\})\leq 1.

Rearranging this equation shows that for
M\geq 3
,
m\leq 4^{M-1}(1+\varepsilon^{-1})^{\frac{M-1}{2}}
.
\Box

S.6.3
Anti-Concentration Bounds

We begin the section by stating two Lemmas that are used in the proof of Theorem
1
. Recall the definition of
T^{M,G}
in Section
S.4
, and that

\mathcal{A}(s,\delta):=\sup_{t\geq s}{\mathrm{P}}(|T^{M,G}-t|\leq\delta),

defined above in Equation (
40
).

Lemma 4
.

Suppose conditions (A1) - (A3) hold. There exists a constant
A=A(\alpha,C_{\phi})
such that that if
K\geq 1
,
M\geq 3
,
m\geq 3
, and
\delta\geq\frac{1}{m}
,

\mathcal{A}(s,\delta)\leq\frac{A}{s\wedge 1}\delta M\log\Big(Km\Big).

Lemma 5
.

Suppose conditions (A1) - (A4) hold. There exists a constant
A=A(\alpha,\beta,C_{\phi},C_{2})
that does not depend on
K
,
n
,
M
,
s
or
\delta
such that if
K\geq 1
,
M\geq 1
,
m\geq 3
,
\delta\geq\frac{1}{m}
,

\mathcal{A}(s,\delta)\leq\frac{A}{(s\wedge 1)^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{3\beta}{2\alpha-1}}}\delta\log^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{3\beta}{2\alpha-1}}(K+1)\log^{\frac{3\beta}{2\alpha-1}}(m).

In the rest of this section we state anti-concentration bounds for Gaussian maxima,
L^{\infty}-L^{2}
mixed norms and
\varepsilon
-nets for the sets
E_{t}
.
Recall Nazarov’s inequality (see
Chernozhukov et al. 2017b
):

Lemma 6
.

Let
X\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
(
p\geq 1
), be a centered gaussian with positive semi-definite covariance matrix
\Sigma
. If
\min_{j}\sqrt{\Sigma_{jj}}\geq\sigma_{\min}
,

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{j=1}^{p}X_{j}\in[t,t+\delta]\Big)\leq\delta\frac{\sqrt{2\log(p)}+2}{\sigma_{\min}}.

(81)

Our proof of the following bound is mainly from
Belloni and
Oliveira 2018
.

Lemma 7
.

Let
X\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
(
p\geq 2)
, be a centered gaussian with positive semi-definite covariance matrix
\Sigma
. For any
s>0
and any
\delta>0
,

\sup_{t\geq s}{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\Big|\max_{j=1}^{p}X_{j}-t\Big|\leq\delta\Big)\leq\frac{84}{5}\frac{\delta}{s}\log(p).

We will also use the following bound which appears in
Chernozhukov et al. 2015
as Theorem 3 (ii). Dependence of the constant on
\sigma_{\max}
and
\sigma_{\min}
is easily determined by inspecting the proof in
Chernozhukov et al. 2015
; note that the bound behaves correctly under rescaling of
X
.

Lemma 8
(Theorem 3 (ii) in
Chernozhukov et al. 2015
).

Let
X\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
(
p\geq 2)
, be a centered Gaussian with covariance matrix
\Sigma
. Let
\sigma_{\min}=\min_{j}\sqrt{\Sigma_{jj}}\leq\max_{j}\sqrt{\Sigma_{jj}}=\sigma_{\max}
. Then for every
\delta>0
,

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{P}}\Big(|\max_{j=1}^{p}X_{j}-t|\leq\delta\Big)\leq C\frac{\sigma_{\max}}{\sigma_{\min}^{2}}\delta\Big\{\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{j=1}^{p}\Big(\frac{X_{j}}{\sqrt{\Sigma_{jj}}}\Big)\Big]+\sqrt{1\vee\log\Big(\frac{\sigma_{\min}}{\delta}\Big)}\Big\},

for a universal constant
C
.

We note that if
W\in(\mathbb{R}^{M})^{K}
is a centered Gaussian, the variable
S=\max_{k=1}^{K}\|W_{k}\|_{2}
is absolutely continuous.

Lemma 9
(Anti-Concentration for Mixed Norms of Gaussian Variables).

Let
W\in(\mathbb{R}^{M})^{K}
be a centered gaussian with positive semi-definite covariance matrix

{\mathrm{P}}\Big(S\in[t-\delta,t]\Big)\leq\frac{51}{2}\frac{\delta+\varepsilon}{t}M\log(K(1+(t-\delta)\varepsilon^{-1})).

(82)

For each
k
let
\Sigma_{k}:=\Var(\pi_{k}W)
, and suppose

\max_{k=1}^{K}\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{M}(\Sigma_{k})_{jj}}\leq U.

(83)

If in addition to the conditions above we assume
\varepsilon<2/\exp(1)
then

{\mathrm{P}}\Big(S\in[t-\delta,t]\Big)\leq 51\Big\{\frac{\delta+\varepsilon}{t}M\log\Big(\frac{4(K_{\Psi_{1}}\vee 1)^{\frac{1}{2}}K(U\vee 1)}{\varepsilon}\Big)\Big\}\vee\varepsilon,

(84)

where

is the constant from Lemma

(ii).

Lemma 10
(Improved Anti-Concentration for Mixed Norms of Gaussian Variables).

Let
\alpha

,

C_{2}j^{-\beta}\leq\sigma_{k,j}\leq C_{1}j^{-\alpha}.

(85)

(86)

Corollary 2

Under the conditions of Lemma

there is a universal constant

that depends only on

and

(87)

.

(89)

.

Proof of Lemma
4

Recall the notation introduced in Section
S.4
.
Let
g\in\mathbb{R}^{MK}
be a centered Gaussian with

, so that

. Recalling Definition

we see that

\mathcal{A}(s,\delta)=\sup_{t\geq s}{\mathrm{P}}(g\in E_{\sqrt{t+\delta}}/E_{\sqrt{t-\delta}})=\sup_{t\geq s}{\mathrm{P}}(S^{2}\in[t-\delta,t+\delta])

where
S:=\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}g\|_{2}
.

If
\delta\geq(s\wedge 1)/10
, we have,

\mathcal{A}(s,\delta)\leq 10\frac{\delta}{s\wedge 1},

and so the bound holds with
A=10
, noting that
M\log\big(Kn\big)\geq 1
. If
\delta<(s\wedge 1)/10
, let
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{m}
and note that

\frac{2(s\wedge 1)-3\delta}{2\sqrt{(s\wedge 1)-\delta}}\geq\frac{2(s\wedge 1)-(3/10)(s\wedge 1)}{2\sqrt{(s\wedge 1)}}\geq\frac{17}{20}\sqrt{s\wedge 1}>\frac{17}{20}\sqrt{10}\sqrt{\delta}\geq\frac{1}{m}=\varepsilon,

where we used the assumption
\delta\geq\frac{1}{m}
.
We will apply Corollary
1
. Note that the sequence
U_{m}:=C_{2,1}C_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha-1}}
satisfies (
83
), where
C_{2,1}
is the constant from Lemma
42
(ii): under (A2), for all

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{M}[\Var(\pi_{k}g)]_{jj}=\sum_{j=1}^{M}\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Var(\Phi_{i,k})/n\Big]_{jj}\leq C_{2,1}^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\|\phi^{f_{k}}_{j}\|_{\Psi_{1}}^{2}\leq C_{2,1}^{2}C_{\phi}^{2}\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha-1}.

Plugging in the lower bounds assumed on
\delta
(i.e.,
\delta\geq 1/m=:\varepsilon
)gives:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(s,\delta)\leq\Big[102\frac{4\delta}{s\wedge 1}M\log\Big(C_{2}Kn\Big\{\Big(C_{2,1}C_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha-1}}\Big)\vee 1\Big\}\Big)\Big]\vee\frac{1}{m}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\frac{\delta}{s\wedge 1}M\log\Big(Km\Big).

where
C_{1}
is a constant that depends only on
U_{n}
, and therefore only on
\alpha
and
C_{\phi}
. To pull the constant out of the argument of the logarithm and absorb it into
C_{1}
, we used

, assumed in the statement of the lemma.
C_{2}
is the universal constant given by Corollary
1
, i.e.
C_{2}:=4(K_{\Psi_{1}}\vee 1)^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Set

.

Proof of Lemma
5

If (A1)-(A4) hold, the random variable
g\in\mathbb{R}^{MK}
, a centered Gaussian with
\Var(g)=\Sigma^{\Phi}
, satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
10
. The lower bound assumption holds directly by (A4) and the upper bound holds by (A2) and Lemma
42
(ii) applied with

and

. Since
T^{M,G}\overset{d}{=}\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}g\|_{2}^{2}
,

\mathcal{A}(s,\delta)=\sup_{t\geq s}{\mathrm{P}}(S^{2}\in[t-\delta,t+\delta])

where
S:=\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}g\|_{2}
. The result follows from the bound of Corollary
2
.
