) to obtain the first inequality, and Lemma
18
to obtain the second inequality.
\Box

Proof of Proposition
1
and Proposition
2

If
\varepsilon\geq s/2
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq s}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}Y\|_{2}\leq t\Big)-{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}X\|_{2}\leq t\Big)\Big|\leq 2\frac{\varepsilon}{s}\leq 2\frac{\varepsilon}{s\wedge 1},

and so the bound of Proposition
1
holds with
C=2
. For the remainder of the proof, assume
\varepsilon<s/2
.
For any
t_{*}\geq s
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq s}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}Y\|_{2}\leq t\Big)-{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}X\|_{2}\leq t\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\geq t_{*}}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}Y\|_{2}\leq t\Big)-{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}X\|_{2}\leq t\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\sup_{t\in[s,t_{*}]}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}Y\|_{2}\leq t\Big)-{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}X\|_{2}\leq t\Big)\Big|.

By Lemma
37
, there is a universal constant
D_{1}
such that if
t_{*}=D_{1}\sqrt{\log(K+1)}U\sqrt{\log(\frac{4}{\varepsilon})}
and
t\geq t_{*}
,

\Big|{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}Y\|_{2}\leq t\Big)-{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}X\|_{2}\leq t\Big)\Big|\leq\varepsilon.

By Lemma
12
,

\sup_{t\in[s,t_{*}]}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}Y\|_{2}\leq t\Big)-{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}X\|_{2}\leq t\Big)\Big|\leq\sup_{t\in[s,t_{*}]}I_{2}(t,\varepsilon)+\sup_{t\in[s,t_{*}]}I_{1}(t,\varepsilon),

(136)

where

\displaystyle I_{1}(t,\varepsilon)=\max_{w\in\{t-\varepsilon,t\}}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}}v^{\top}X\leq w\Big)-{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}}v^{\top}Y\leq w\Big)\Big|,

\displaystyle I_{2}(t,\varepsilon)=\Big|{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{v\in V_{\varepsilon,t}}v^{\top}Y\leq t\Big)-{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{v\in V_{\varepsilon,t}}v^{\top}Y\leq t-\varepsilon\Big)\Big|.

Therefore

\sup_{t\geq s}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}Y\|_{2}\leq t\Big)-{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}X\|_{2}\leq t\Big)\Big|\leq\sup_{t\in[s,t_{*}]}I_{2}(t,\varepsilon)+\sup_{t\in[s,t_{*}]}I_{1}(t,\varepsilon)+\varepsilon.

We now introduce some additional notation. Let
f:\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}\rightarrow\{1,\dots,|\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}|\}\subset\mathbb{N}
be a bijection and define
\widetilde{Y},\widetilde{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}|}
component-wise as follows: for each
1\leq\ell\leq|\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}|
let
[\widetilde{Y}]_{\ell}=(f^{-1}(\ell))^{\top}Y
and
[\widetilde{X}]_{\ell}=(f^{-1}(\ell))^{\top}X
.
Note that

I_{1}(t,\varepsilon)=\max_{w\in\{t-\varepsilon,t\}}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{\ell=1}^{|\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}|}[\widetilde{X}]_{\ell}\leq w\Big)-{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{\ell=1}^{|\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}|}[\widetilde{Y}]_{\ell}\leq w\Big)\Big|,

and

I_{2}(t,\varepsilon)={\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{\ell=1}^{|\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}|}[\widetilde{Y}]_{\ell}\leq t\Big)-{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{\ell=1}^{|\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}|}[\widetilde{Y}]_{\ell}\leq t-\varepsilon\Big)\leq{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\Big|\max_{\ell=1}^{|\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}|}[\widetilde{Y}]_{\ell}-t\Big|\leq\varepsilon\Big).

Assume that

\displaystyle\sup_{w\geq s/4}{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\big|\max_{\ell=1}^{|\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}|}[\widetilde{Y}]_{\ell}-w\big|\leq\tau\Big)\leq D_{m}(\tau\vee 1/{m}),

Under this assumption it follows from the definition of
I_{2}(t,\varepsilon)
that

\sup_{t\in[s,t_{*}]}I_{2}(t,\varepsilon)\leq D_{m}(\varepsilon\vee{m}^{-1}).

To deal with
I_{1}
, apply Lemma
20
to obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[s,t_{*}]}I_{1}(t,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\in[s/2,t_{*}]}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{\ell=1}^{|\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}|}[\widetilde{X}]_{\ell}\leq w\Big)-{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{\ell=1}^{|\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}|}[\widetilde{Y}]_{\ell}\leq w\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq D_{8}\sup_{t\in[s/2,t_{*}]}\Big(D_{m}^{2/3}\vee\frac{1}{s\wedge 1}\Big)\big({\big\{}\log\big(|V_{\varepsilon,t}|\big)\|\Var(\widetilde{Y})-\Var(\widetilde{Z})\|_{\infty}{\big\}}{\vee{m}^{-2}}\big)^{1/3}.

To bound the difference of the variances, we argue as follows. Since each
v\in\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}
has at most
M
non-zero entries and satisfies
\|v\|_{2}=1
, we have for
v,w\in\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}

|v^{\top}(\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2})w|\leq\|v\|_{1}\|(\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2})w\|_{\infty}\leq\|v\|_{1}\|\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2}\|_{\infty}\|w\|_{1}\leq M\|\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2}\|_{\infty},

(137)

(we used the inequality
\|v\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{M}\|v\|_{2}
which follows by Hölder’s inequality), we obtain

\|\Var(\widetilde{Y})-\Var(\widetilde{Z})\|_{\infty}\leq M\|\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2}\|_{\infty}.

Thus

\sup_{t\in[s,t_{*}]}I_{1}(t,\varepsilon)\leq D_{8}\Big(D_{m}^{2/3}\vee\frac{1}{s\wedge 1}\Big)\sup_{t\in[s/2,t_{*}]}\Big(\big\{M\log\big(|V_{\varepsilon,t}|\big)\|\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2}\|_{\infty}\big\}\vee{m}^{-2}\Big)^{1/3}.

By Lemma
1

\sup_{t\in[s/2,t_{*}]}\log\big(|V_{\varepsilon,t}|\big)\leq D_{10}M\log(K(1+t_{*}\varepsilon^{-1}))

for a universal constant
D_{10}
. Then note that

\displaystyle\log\Big(K\big(1+t_{*}/\varepsilon\big)\Big)=\log\Big(K\big(1+D_{1}\sqrt{\log(K+1)}U\big[\log(\frac{4}{\varepsilon})\big]^{1/2}/\varepsilon\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\log\Big(K\big(D_{5}K(U\vee 1)\varepsilon^{-2}\big)\Big)\leq D_{6}(1+\log(U\vee 1))\log\big(K\varepsilon^{-1}\big).

for a universal constants
D_{5}
and
D_{6}
(we used
K/\varepsilon>\exp(1)
) giving the bound

\sup_{t\in[s,t_{*}]}I_{1}(t,\varepsilon)\leq D_{11}\Big(D_{m}^{2/3}\vee\frac{1}{s\wedge 1}\Big)\Big(\big\{M^{2}(1+\log(U\vee 1))\log\big(K\varepsilon^{-1}\big)\|\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2}\|_{\infty}\big\}\vee{m}^{-2}\Big)^{1/3}.

To complete the proof of Proposition
1
, apply Lemma
7
to obtain for any
\tau>0
and any
{m}\geq 3
and for any
s
and
\varepsilon
satisfying
\varepsilon<s
(so that
\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}
is defined for all
t\geq s
)

\sup_{w\geq s/4}{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\big|\max_{\ell=1}^{|\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}|}[\widetilde{Y}]_{\ell}-w\big|\leq\tau\Big)\leq\frac{17}{s/4}\log(|\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}|)\tau\leq\frac{68}{s\wedge 1}\log(|\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}|)\tau\leq D_{m}(\tau\vee 1/{m}),

(138)

for

D_{m}=\frac{68}{s\wedge 1}\log(|\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}|)\leq\frac{D_{12}}{s\wedge 1}M(1+\log(U\vee 1))\log\big(K\varepsilon^{-1}\big).

Thus

\sup_{t\in[s,t_{*}]}I_{1}(t,\varepsilon)\leq\frac{D_{14}}{s\wedge 1}M^{4/3}(1+\log(U\vee 1))\log\big(K\varepsilon^{-1}\big)\|\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2}\|_{\infty}^{1/3},

where the term with
\vee m^{-2}
can be dropped because
n
can be chosen arbitrarily large in light of (
138
).
Combined with the earlier bounds established in this proof

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq s}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}Y\|_{2}\leq t\Big)-{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}X\|_{2}\leq t\Big)\Big|\leq\sup_{t\in[s,t_{*}]}I_{2}(t,\varepsilon)+\sup_{t\in[s,t_{*}]}I_{1}(t,\varepsilon)+\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\frac{D_{15}(1+\log(U\vee 1))}{s\wedge 1}M\log\big(K\varepsilon^{-1}\big)\Big(M^{1/3}\|\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2}\|_{\infty}^{1/3}+\varepsilon\Big)+\varepsilon.

The final
+\varepsilon
can be dropped by enlarging the constant since
M\log(K/\varepsilon)\geq 1
and this completes the proof of Proposition
1
.

We now move to the proof of Proposition
2
. Here, we apply Lemma
11
to obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{w\geq s/4}{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\big|\max_{\ell=1}^{|\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}|}[\widetilde{Y}]_{\ell}-w\big|\leq\tau\Big)=\sup_{w\geq s/4}{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon,t}}v^{\top}Y\in[w-\tau,w+\tau]\Big)

\displaystyle\leq D_{\alpha,\beta,s/4,C_{1},C_{2}}(\tau\vee{m}^{-1})\log^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{3\beta}{2\alpha-1}}(K+1)\log^{\frac{3\beta}{2\alpha-1}}(n)=D_{{m}}(\tau\vee{m}^{-1})

for
D_{m}=D_{\alpha,\beta,s/4,C_{1},C_{2}}\log^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{3\beta}{2\alpha-1}}(K+1)\log^{\frac{3\beta}{2\alpha-1}}({m})
. Combining this with the bounds obtained earlier yields

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq s}\Big|{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}Y\|_{2}\leq t\Big)-{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}X\|_{2}\leq t\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\in[s,t_{*}]}I_{2}(t,\varepsilon)+\sup_{t\in[s,t_{*}]}I_{1}(t,\varepsilon)+\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\frac{D_{16}(1+\log(U\vee 1))^{1/3}}{s\wedge 1}\log^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{3\beta}{2\alpha-1}}(K+1)\log^{\frac{3\beta}{2\alpha-1}}({m})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\Big(M^{2/3}\|\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2}\|_{\infty}^{1/3}\log^{1/3}\big(K\varepsilon^{-1}\big)+(\varepsilon\vee{m}^{-2/3})\Big),

for sufficiently large constant
D_{16}
, noting the bound

\big\{M^{2/3}\|\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2}\|_{\infty}^{1/3}\log^{1/3}\big(K\varepsilon^{-1}\big)\}\vee{m}^{-2/3}\leq M^{2/3}\|\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2}\|_{\infty}^{1/3}\log^{1/3}\big(K\varepsilon^{-1}\big)+{m}^{-2/3}.

\Box

S.6.9
Remainder Bounds

We begin this section by stating Lemmas that are used in the proof of Theorems
1
and
2
. Recall the notation introduced in Assumptions
1
, and Section
S.4
, and let
K_{\phi,\alpha}=C_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha-1}}
, and recall the definition of the conditional Orlicz norm given in Definition
5
.

Lemma 21
.

Recall the definition of
T^{M}
and the definition of
T^{d}
made in Section
S.4
. Assume that (A1) and (A2) hold. There exists a universal constant
C
such that for any
M\geq 1
and
K\geq 1
,

\|T^{M}-T^{d}\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq C(q^{-1}C_{H}^{2}+C_{H}K_{\phi,\alpha}+K_{\phi,\alpha}^{2})[\log(K+1)]^{2}[T^{-q}+M^{1-2\alpha}].

(139)

Lemma 22
.

Recall the definition of
T^{M,e}
and the definition of
T^{d,e}
made in Section
S.4
. Assume that (A1) and (A2) hold.There exists a universal constant
C
and a random variable
Y\in\sigma(f)
such that for all
M\geq 1
and
K\geq 1
,

\|T^{M,e}-T^{d,e}\|_{\Psi_{1}|\sigma(f)}\leq Y,

(140)

and

\|Y\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\overset{a.s}{\leq}C(q^{-1}C_{H}^{2}+C_{H}K_{\phi,\alpha}+K_{\phi,\alpha}^{2})[\log(K+1)]^{3}[T^{-q}+M^{1-2\alpha}].

(141)

The rest of this section contains results that are used to prove Lemmas
21
and
22
.

Note that Lemma
21
follows straightforwardly from Lemmas
23
and Lemmas
24
, using Lemma
40
to combine the two bounds. Likewise, Lemma
22
follows straightforwardly from Lemma
25
and Lemmas
26
.

Lemma 23
.

Recall the definition of
T^{M}
in Section
S.4
. Assume that (A2) holds. There exists a universal constant
C
such that for any
M\geq 1
, and any
K\geq 1
,

\Big\|\max_{k=1}^{K}\int_{I}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}-T^{M}\Big\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq CK^{2}_{\phi,\alpha}\log^{2}(K+1)M^{1-2\alpha}.

(142)

Additionally,

\Big\|\max_{k=1}^{K}\int_{I}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}\Big\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq CK^{2}_{\phi,\alpha}\log^{2}(K+1).

Lemma 24
.

Recall the definition of
T^{d}
in Section
S.4
. Assume (A1), (A2) hold. There exists a constant
C
that does not depend on
n
,
M
,
K
,
T
,
\alpha
,
q
,
C_{H}
or
C_{\phi}
such that for all
K\geq 1
,

\Big\|\max_{k=1}^{K}\int_{I}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}-T^{d}\Big\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq C(q^{-1}C_{H}^{2}+C_{H}K_{\phi,\alpha})\frac{\log^{2}(K+1)}{T^{q}}.

(143)

Lemma 25
.

Recall the definition of
T^{d,e}
in Section
S.4
. There exists a constant
C
that does not depend on
n
,
M
,
K
,
T
,
\alpha
,
q
,
C_{H}
or
C_{\phi}
, and a random variable
Y\in\sigma(f)
such that for all
K\geq 1

\Big\|\max_{k=1}^{K}\int_{I}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[f_{k,i}-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}]}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}-T^{e,d}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}|\sigma(f)}\leq Y,

and

\|Y\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\overset{a.s}{\leq}C(q^{-1}C_{H}^{2}+C_{H}K_{\phi,\alpha})\frac{\log^{3}(K+1)}{T^{q}}.

Lemma 26
.

Recall the definition of
T^{M,e}
in Section
S.4
. There exists a constant
C
that does not depend on
n
,
M
,
K
,
T
,
\alpha
,
q
,
C_{H}
or
C_{\phi}
, and a random variable
W\in\sigma(f)
such that for all
K\geq 1
,

\Big\|T^{e,M}-\max_{k=1}^{K}\int_{I}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[f_{k,i}-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}]}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}|\sigma(f)}\leq W

almost surely, and

\|W\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq CK^{2}_{\phi,\alpha}M^{1-2\alpha}\log^{3}(K+1).

Proof of Lemma
23

The definition of
T^{M}
and the
L^{\infty}
triangle inequality give the point-wise bound

\displaystyle\Big|\max_{k=1}^{K}\int_{I}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}-T^{M}\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\max_{k=1}^{K}\int_{I}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}-\max_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi^{f_{k,i}}_{j}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{k=1}^{K}\Big|\int_{I}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}-\sum_{j=1}^{M}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi^{f_{k,i}}_{j}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}\Big|.

Let
K_{f_{k,i}}
be the covariance kernel of
f_{k,i}
. The identity

K_{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}K_{f_{k,i}}

holds and so the operator inequality

\mathcal{K}_{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{K}_{f_{k,i}}}{n}

(see Theorem
5
for the definition of
\mathcal{K}_{X}
for a stochastic process
X
) is seen to hold by linearity.

Thus we have the equality in
L^{2}
(see Theorem
6
for the definition of
Z^{X}_{j}
in terms of
\mathcal{K}_{X}
)

Z^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}}_{j}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z^{f_{k,i}}_{j}}{n}.

Hence by linearity, for each
1\leq j<\infty
(see Theorem
6
for a definition of the linear map
X\xrightarrow{L_{Z^{X}_{j}}}\phi^{X}_{j}
.),

\phi^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}}_{j}\overset{a.s}{=}\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi^{f_{k,i}}_{j}}{\sqrt{n}}.

By Lemma
29
, part (i),

\int_{I}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}d\mu-\sum_{j=1}^{M}\Big(\phi^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}}_{j}\Big)^{2}=\sum_{j=M+1}^{\infty}\Big(\phi^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}}_{j}\Big)^{2},

and so almost surely,

\int_{I}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}d\mu-\sum_{j=1}^{M}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi^{f_{k,i}}_{j}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}=\sum_{j=M+1}^{\infty}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi^{f_{k,i}}_{j}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}.

We have

\displaystyle\Big\|\int_{I}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}d\mu-\sum_{j=1}^{M}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi^{f_{k,i}}_{j}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}\Big\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq C_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\sum_{j=M+1}^{\infty}\Big\|\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi^{f_{k,i}}_{j}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}\Big\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}.

\displaystyle\leq K_{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\sum_{j=M+1}^{\infty}\max_{i=1}^{n}\Big\|(\phi^{f_{k,i}}_{j})^{2}\Big\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq K_{p}C_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\sum_{j=M+1}^{\infty}\max_{i=1}^{n}\|\phi^{f_{k,i}}_{j}\|_{\Psi_{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq K_{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}C_{\phi}^{2}\sum_{j=M+1}^{\infty}j^{-2\alpha}\leq K_{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}C_{\phi}^{2}\frac{M^{1-2\alpha}}{2\alpha-1},

where the first inequality is by
Lemma
29
, part (ii), the second by Lemma
41
(iii), the third by Lemma
42
, the fourth by condition (A2) of Assumptions
1
, and the last by the first statement of Lemma
34
. Then by Lemma
41
(ii), recalling the first inequality in the proof,

\Big\|\max_{k=1}^{K}\int_{I}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}d\mu-T^{M}\Big\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq K_{\Psi_{1}}^{2}K_{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\log^{2}(K+1)C_{\phi}^{2}\frac{M^{1-2\alpha}}{2\alpha-1}.

Set
C=K_{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}K_{\Psi_{1}}^{2}
. This completes the proof of the first bound in the Lemma.

The second bound is proved similarly after observing that

\int_{I}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}d\mu=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\Big(\phi^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}}_{j}\Big)^{2}.

Now proceed similarly to the proof of the first statement of the Lemma, using the second statement of Lemma
34
to obtain

\Bigg\|\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\Big(\phi^{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}}_{j}\Big)^{2}\Bigg\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq(K_{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}})^{1/2}C_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha-1}}.

(144)

The second statement in the Lemma then follows from an application of Lemma
42
(i) and Lemma
41
(ii).

\Box

Proof of Lemma
24

Define
S_{k}(x)=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}(x)}{\sqrt{n}}
to lighten notation. By definition of
T^{d}
and the
L^{\infty}
triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\Big|\max_{k=1}^{K}\int_{I}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}-T^{d}\Big|=\Big|\max_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}S_{k}^{2}(x)dx-\max_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}S_{k}(t_{\ell})^{2}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{k=1}^{K}\Big|\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}S_{k}^{2}(x)dx-\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}S_{k}(t_{\ell})^{2}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}|S_{k}(x)-S_{k}(t_{\ell})||S_{k}(x)+S_{k}(t_{\ell})|dx,

For each
\ell
and
k
, and each
x\in[t_{\ell-1},t_{\ell}]

\displaystyle\Big\||S_{k}(x)-S_{k}(t_{\ell})||S_{k}(x)+S_{k}(t_{\ell})|\Big\|_{\widetilde{\Psi}_{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq\Big\||S_{k}(x)-S_{k}(t_{\ell})||S_{k}(x)+S_{k}(t_{\ell})|\Big\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq\|S_{k}(x)-S_{k}(t_{\ell})\|_{\Psi_{1}}\|S_{k}(x)+S_{k}(t_{\ell})\|_{\Psi_{1}}

Lemma
42

\displaystyle\leq K_{1}^{2}\Big(\max_{i=1}^{n}\|f_{k,i}(x)-f_{k,i}(t_{\ell})\|_{\Psi_{1}}\Big)\Big(\max_{i=1}^{n}\|f_{k,i}(x)+f_{k,i}(t_{\ell})\|_{\Psi_{1}}\Big)

Lemma
41
(iii)

\displaystyle\leq K_{1}^{2}C_{H}T^{-q}\max_{i=1}^{n}\|f_{k,i}(x)+f_{k,i}(t_{\ell})\|_{\Psi_{1}}

Assumption (A1)

\displaystyle\leq 2K_{1}^{2}C_{H}T^{-q}\max_{i=1}^{n}\Big\|\sup_{s\in[t_{\ell-1},t_{\ell}]}f_{k,i}(s)\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}

Triangle ineq. and monotonicity

\displaystyle\leq 2K_{1}^{2}C_{H}T^{-(q+1)}\max_{i=1}^{n}\Big\|\sup_{s\in[0,1]}f_{k,i}(s)\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}

Supremum over larger set

\displaystyle\leq CK_{1}^{2}K_{*}C_{H}T^{-q}\Big(\frac{C_{H}}{q}+C_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha-1}}\Big)

Lemma
33

for a universal constant
C
. The use of Lemma
33
is justified by (
144
). Thus we have

\displaystyle\Big\|\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}|S_{k}(x)-S_{k}(t_{\ell})||S_{k}(x)+S_{k}(t_{\ell})|dx\Big\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\Big\|\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}|S_{k}(x)-S_{k}(t_{\ell})||S_{k}(x)+S_{k}(t_{\ell})|dx\Big\|_{\widetilde{\Psi}_{\frac{1}{2}}}

Lemma
39

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}2K_{1}^{2}K_{*}C_{H}T^{-q}\Big(\frac{C_{H}}{q}+C_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha-1}}\Big)dx

bound above and Lemma
43

\displaystyle\leq C_{T^{d}}T^{-q-1}\Big(q^{-1}C_{H}^{2}+C_{H}C_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha-1}}\Big)

where
C_{T^{d}}
is a universal constant. We may now apply the triangle inequality up to the constant
C_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}
to yield, for each
k
,

\Big\|\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}S_{k}^{2}(x)dx-\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}S_{k}(t_{\ell})^{2}\Big\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq C_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}C_{T^{d}}T^{-q}\Big(q^{-1}C_{H}^{2}+C_{H}C_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha-1}}\Big).

(145)

Thus by Lemma
41
part (ii)

\displaystyle\Big\|\max_{k=1}^{K}\int_{I}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{k,i}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}d\mu-T^{d}\Big\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq K_{\Psi_{1}}^{2}\log^{2}(K+1)C_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}C_{T^{d}}T^{-q}\Big(q^{-1}C_{H}^{2}+C_{H}C_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha-1}}\Big).

Setting
C
equal to
K_{\Psi_{1}}^{2}C_{T^{d}}C_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}
finishes the proof.
\Box

Proof of Lemma
25

Recalling the definition of
T^{d,e}
, we need to bound

\displaystyle\Big|\max_{k=1}^{K}\int_{0}^{1}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[f_{k,i}(x)-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(x)]}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}dx-\max_{k=1}^{K}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[f_{k,i}(t_{\ell})-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(t_{\ell})]}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{k=1}^{K}\Big|\int_{0}^{1}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[f_{k,i}(x)-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(x)]}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}dx-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[f_{k,i}(t_{\ell})-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(t_{\ell})]}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{k=1}^{K}\Big|\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\Big\{\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[f_{k,i}(x)-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(x)]}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}dx-\frac{1}{T}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[f_{k,i}(t_{\ell})-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(t_{\ell})]}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}\Big\}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\max_{k=1}^{K}\Big|\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}\Big\{\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[f_{k,i}(x)-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(x)]\Big)^{2}-\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[f_{k,i}(t_{\ell})-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(t_{\ell})]\Big)^{2}\Big\}dx\Big|

\displaystyle=:g(f,e)

where
f
stands for the collection
(f_{k,i})_{i=1,\dots,n,k=1,\dots,K}
and
e\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
has entries
e_{i}
.
We introduce the following notation to decrease the typographical length of the remaining argument. Let
A_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
B_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be defined coordinate wise for
x\in[t_{\ell-1},t_{\ell}]
as follows:

\displaystyle[A_{\ell,f_{k}}]_{i}(x)=[(f_{k,i}(x)-f_{k,i}(t_{\ell}))-(\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(x)-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(t_{\ell}))].

\displaystyle[B_{\ell,f_{k}}]_{i}(x)=[(f_{k,i}(x)+f_{k,i}(t_{\ell}))-(\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(x)+\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(t_{\ell}))].

Applying the difference of squares formula now gives:

\displaystyle\Big|\max_{k=1}^{K}\int_{I}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[f_{k,i}(x)-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(x)]}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}dx-T^{d,e}\Big|\leq\frac{1}{n}\max_{k=1}^{K}\Big|\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}\langle e,A_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\rangle\langle e,B_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\rangle dx\Big|

Hence it suffices to estimate
\|\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}\langle e,A_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\rangle\langle e,B_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\rangle dx\|_{\Psi_{1}|\sigma(f)}
, using monotonicity of the conditional norm. We will now use Definition
5
with
X=f,Y=e
and
g
as defined earlier in this proof. Note that the definition applies in our setting because the functions
f_{k,i}
are independent of the Gaussian variables
e
.

\displaystyle\Big[\Big\|\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}\langle e,A_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\rangle\langle e,B_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\rangle dx\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}|\sigma(f)}\Big](\omega)

\displaystyle\overset{a.s}{=}\Big\|\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}\langle e,A_{\ell,f^{\omega}_{k}}(x)\rangle\langle e,B_{\ell,f^{\omega}_{k}}(x)\rangle dx\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\Big\|\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}\langle e,A_{\ell,f^{\omega}_{k}}(x)\rangle\langle e,B_{\ell,f^{\omega}_{k}}(x)\rangle dx\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}

where the notation
f^{\omega}_{k}
refers to a fixed realization of the random functions
\{f_{k,i}\}_{i=1,k=1}^{n,K}
. This implies that for any
\omega
the variables
\langle e,A_{\ell,f^{\omega}_{k}}(x)\rangle
and
\langle e,B_{\ell,f^{\omega}_{k}}(x)\rangle
are Gaussian random variables with standard deviations
\|A_{\ell,f^{\omega}_{k}}(x)\|_{2}
and
\|B_{\ell,f^{\omega}_{k}}(x)\|_{2}
respectively. Elementary computations show that if
Z\sim N(0,\sigma^{2})
, then
\|Z\|_{\psi_{2}}=\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}\sigma
. Thus,

\displaystyle\Big\|\langle e,A_{\ell,f^{\omega}_{k}}(x)\rangle\langle e,B_{\ell,f^{\omega}_{k}}(x)\rangle\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\Big\|\langle e,A_{\ell,f^{\omega}_{k}}(x)\rangle\Big\|_{\Psi_{2}}\Big\|\langle e,B_{\ell,f^{\omega}_{k}}(x)\rangle\Big\|_{\Psi_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8}{3}\|A_{\ell,f^{\omega}_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\|B_{\ell,f^{\omega}_{k}}(x)\|_{2}.

By continuity of each
f^{\omega}_{k,i}
, the above is a continuous function of
x
, thus Riemann integrable. An application of Lemma
43
yields

\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\Big\|\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}\langle e,A_{\ell,f^{\omega}_{k}}(x)\rangle\langle e,B_{\ell,f^{\omega}_{k}}(x)\rangle dx\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}\leq\frac{8}{3}\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}\|A_{\ell,f^{\omega}_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\|B_{\ell,f^{\omega}_{k}}(x)\|_{2}dx

and so

\displaystyle\Big\|\max_{k=1}^{K}\int_{I}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[f_{k,i}-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}]}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}-T^{d,e}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}|\sigma(f)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8}{3}K_{\Psi_{1}}\frac{\log(K+1)}{n}\max_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}\|A_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\|B_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}dx.

We define
Y
to be equal to the right-hand-side of this inequality, i.e.

\displaystyle Y:=\frac{8}{3}K_{\Psi_{1}}\frac{\log(K+1)}{n}\max_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}\|A_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\|B_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}dx,

and proceed to compute the
\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}
norm of
Y
. To this end we first bound the
\widetilde{\Psi}_{1/2}
norm of the integrand. By Lemma
42
(ii) applied for
p=1/2
,

\Big\|\|A_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\|B_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\Big\|_{\widetilde{\Psi}_{1/2}}\leq\Big\|\|A_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\|B_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1/2}}\leq\Big\|\|A_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}\Big\|\|B_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}.

We will now estimate
\|\|A_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\|_{\Psi_{1}}
and
\|\|B_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\|_{\Psi_{1}}
separately. Note that by the Euclidean triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\|A_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(f_{k,i}(x)-f_{k,i}(t_{\ell}))^{2}}+\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(x)-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(t_{\ell}))^{2}},

and by Lemma
40
(triangle inequality for the
\Psi_{1}
norm),

\displaystyle\|\|A_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\|_{\Psi_{1}}\leq\Big\|\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(f_{k,i}(x)-f_{k,i}(t_{\ell}))^{2}}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}+\Big\|\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(x)-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(t_{\ell}))^{2}}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}.

To even further lighten notation, let
D_{k,i,\ell}(x)=f_{k,i}(x)-f_{k,i}(t_{\ell})
be defined on
[t_{\ell-1},t_{\ell}]
. To shorten notation we drop the argument
x
in
D_{k,i,\ell}
below. We have,

\displaystyle\Big\|\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(f_{k,i}(x)-f_{k,i}(t_{\ell}))^{2}}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}=\Big\|\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(D_{k,i,\ell})^{2}}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}=\sqrt{\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(D_{k,i,\ell})^{2}\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}}

Lemma
42
(ii)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}[(D_{k,i,\ell})^{2}-\mathbb{E}[(D_{k,i,\ell})^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[(D_{k,i,\ell})^{2}]\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}[(D_{k,i,\ell})^{2}-\mathbb{E}[(D_{k,i,\ell})^{2}]\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}}+\sqrt{\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[(D_{k,i,\ell})^{2}]\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}}

Lemma
40

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\sqrt{n}\max_{i}\|(D_{k,i,\ell})^{2}-\mathbb{E}[(D_{k,i,\ell})^{2}]\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}}+\sqrt{n}\max_{i}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[(D_{k,i,\ell})^{2}]}

Lemma
41
(iii) and Lemma
40
.

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\sqrt{n}\max_{i}\|(D_{k,i,\ell})^{2}\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}+\sqrt{n}\max_{i}\mathbb{E}[(D_{k,i,\ell})^{2}]}+\sqrt{n}\max_{i}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[(D_{k,i,\ell})^{2}]}

Lemma
40
.

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{4}}\sqrt{\max_{i}\|(D_{k,i,\ell})^{2}\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}}+\sqrt{n}\max_{i}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[(D_{k,i,\ell})^{2}]}

sub-additivity of square root

\displaystyle\lesssim n^{\frac{1}{4}}\max_{i}\|(D_{k,i,\ell})\|_{\Psi_{1}}+\sqrt{n}\max_{i}\|(D_{k,i,\ell})\|_{\Psi_{1}}

Lemma
42
(i) and (ii).

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{n}C_{H}T^{-q},

(A1) of Assumption
1

where all bounds are uniform
in
x\in[t_{\ell-1},t_{\ell}]
and all constants are universal.
The second piece in the bound on
\|\|A_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\|_{\Psi_{1}}
is of lower order:

\displaystyle\Big\|\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(x)-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(t_{\ell}))^{2}}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}\|\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(x)-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(t_{\ell})\|_{\Psi_{1}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}D_{k,i,\ell}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\max_{i}\|D_{k,i,\ell}\|_{\Psi_{1}}\leq C_{H}T^{-q},

where the first inequality follows from Lemma
41
(iii) and the final bound from Assumption (A1). Therefore by the triangle inequality,

\|\|A_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\|_{\Psi_{1}}\lesssim\sqrt{n}C_{H}T^{-q}.

Moving to the next factor note that

\displaystyle\|B_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(f_{k,i}(x)+f_{k,i}(t_{\ell}))^{2}}+\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(x)+\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}(t_{\ell}))^{2}}.

We will estimate the norm of the first piece in this bound; the second has lower order as can be seen by an argument similar to the one made when bounding the second piece in the upper bound to
\|\|A_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\|_{\Psi_{1}}
. Define
S_{k,i,\ell}(x)=f_{k,i}(x)+f_{k,i}(t_{\ell})
. By similar arguments as in the analysis of
A
,

\displaystyle\Big\|\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(f_{k,i}(x)+f_{k,i}(t_{\ell}))^{2}}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}=\Big\|\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}S_{k,i,\ell}^{2}(x)}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}\lesssim\sqrt{n}\max_{i}\|S_{k,i,\ell}\|_{\Psi_{1}},

where the last inequality follows from an argument similar to the one made when bounding the first piece of the upper bound to
\|\|A_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\|_{\Psi_{1}}
. Finally, for each
i
, by Lemma
33
,

\displaystyle\|S_{k,i,\ell}\|_{\Psi_{1}}\lesssim\|\sup_{s\in[0,1]}f_{k,i}(s)\|_{\Psi_{1}}\lesssim C_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha-1}}+\frac{C_{H}}{q}.

Thus we have proved, uniformly in
x\in[t_{\ell-1},t_{\ell}]

\Big\|\|A_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\|B_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\Big\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim nT^{-q}C_{H}\Big(C_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha-1}}+\frac{C_{H}}{q}\Big).

This upper bound is a constant and thus Riemann integrable function of
x
.
Thus

\displaystyle\Big\|\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}\|A_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\|B_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}dx\Big\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\Big\|\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}\|A_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\|B_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}dx\Big\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}nT^{-q}C_{H}\Big(C_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha-1}}+\frac{C_{H}}{q}\Big)dx

(Lemma
43
)

\displaystyle=nT^{-q}C_{H}\Big(C_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha-1}}+\frac{C_{H}}{q}\Big).

Thus, by the above bound and Lemma
41
part (ii)

\displaystyle\|Y\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\frac{8}{3}K_{\Psi_{1}}\frac{\log(K+1)}{n}\max_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{T}\int_{t_{\ell-1}}^{t_{\ell}}\|A_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}\|B_{\ell,f_{k}}(x)\|_{2}dx\Big\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq CT^{-q}\log^{3}(K+1)C_{H}\Big(C_{\phi}\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha-1}}+\frac{C_{H}}{q}\Big).

for a sufficiently large universal constant
C
.

\Box

Proof of Lemma
26

Define
T^{e,1}
and
S^{e}_{k}
as follows:

T^{e,1}=\max_{k}\int_{I}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[f_{k,i}-\bar{f_{k,\cdot}}]}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}dx,

and

S^{e}_{k}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[f_{k,i}-\bar{f_{k,\cdot}}]}{\sqrt{n}}.

An elementary calculation shows that the process
S^{e}_{k}
admits Karhunen–Loève basis vectors
Z_{j}^{S^{e}_{k}}
equal to
Z^{f_{1,k}}_{j}
. Then,

\displaystyle\phi^{S^{e}_{k}}_{j}=\int_{I}Z^{S^{e}_{k}}_{j}(t)S^{e}_{k}dt=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{I}Z^{f_{1,k}}_{j}(t)e_{i}[f_{k,i}(t)-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}]dt=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[\phi^{f_{k,i}}_{j}-\bar{\phi}^{f_{k,\cdot}}_{j}]}{\sqrt{n}}

where in the last equality we used the definition of each
\phi^{f_{k,i}}
and linearity of the integral with respect to the sum on
i
. We are now ready to compute the norm bounds indicated in the statement of the lemma. Note that

\displaystyle|T^{e}-T^{M,e}|

\displaystyle=\Big|\max_{k}\int_{I}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[f_{k,i}-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}]}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}dx-\max_{k}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{e_{i}[\phi_{j}^{f_{k,i}}-\bar{\phi}_{j}^{f_{k,\cdot}}]}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{k}\Big|\int_{I}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[f_{k,i}-\bar{f}_{k,\cdot}]}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}dx-\sum_{j=1}^{M}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[\phi_{j}^{f_{k,i}}-\bar{\phi}_{j}^{f_{k,\cdot}}]}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}\Big|

\displaystyle=\max_{k}\sum_{j=M+1}^{\infty}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[\phi_{j}^{f_{k,i}}-\bar{\phi}_{j}^{f_{k,\cdot}}]}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle=:g(f,e)

where the second to last equality follows from Lemma
29
, while the inequality follows from the properties of the
\max
function. We will now bound
\|T^{e}-T^{M,e}\|_{\Psi_{1}|\sigma(f)}
.
Recall that (see Definition
5
),

\displaystyle\big[\|T^{e}-T^{M,e}\|_{\Psi_{1}|\sigma(f)}\big](\omega)=\Bigg\|\max_{k}\sum_{j=M+1}^{\infty}\Big(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}[\phi_{j}^{f^{\omega}_{k,i}}-\bar{\phi}_{j}^{f^{\omega}_{k,\cdot}}]}{\sqrt{n}}\Big)^{2}\Bigg\|_{\Psi_{1}},

where
\phi_{j}^{f^{\omega}_{k,i}}:=\int_{I}f_{k,i}(t;\omega)Z_{j}^{f_{1,k}}(t)dt
refer to the
j^{th}
Karhuen-Loeve score of a fixed realization of the
f_{k,i}
. Let

U_{jk}(\omega):=\Big[(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\phi_{j}^{f^{\omega}_{k,i}}-\bar{\phi}_{j}^{f^{\omega}_{k,\cdot}})^{2}\Big]^{1/2}.

For appropriate dependent standard normal variables
Z_{jk}
which are independent of
\sigma(f)
and therefore of
\sigma(\cup_{k=1}^{K}\cup_{j=M+1}^{\infty}U_{jk})
, since the
U_{jk}
are derived from the
f_{k,i}
deterministically,

\big[\|T^{e}-T^{M,e}\|_{\Psi_{1}|\sigma(f)}\big](\omega)=\Big\|\max_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{j=M+1}^{\infty}(U_{jk}(\omega)Z_{jk})^{2}\Big\|_{\psi_{1}}

(146)

Recall that if
Z
is standard normal, then
\|Z\|_{\psi_{2}}
exists and
\|Z^{2}\|_{\Psi_{1}}=(\|Z\|_{\psi_{2}})^{2}=8\Var(Z)/3
. The first inequality follows from Lemma
42
(i) and the second from a standard computation. It follows that from the above derivations, for a universal constant
C
, we have a.s.

\Big\|\sum_{j=M+1}^{\infty}\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}(\phi_{j}^{f_{k,i}}-\bar{\phi}_{j}^{f_{k,\cdot}})\Big)^{2}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}|\sigma(f)}(\omega)\leq\sum_{j=M+1}^{\infty}(C/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\phi_{j}^{f_{k,i}^{\omega}}-\bar{\phi}_{j}^{f_{k,\cdot}^{\omega}})^{2}.

Thus, for a universal constant
C
, by Lemma
41
(ii)

\|T^{e}-T^{e,M}\|_{\Psi_{1}|\sigma(f)}\leq\frac{C}{n}\log(K+1)\max_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{j=M+1}^{\infty}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\phi_{j}^{f_{k,i}}-\bar{\phi}_{j}^{f_{k,\cdot}})^{2}\quad a.s.

(147)

Thus for a universal constant
C
whose values differ from one to line, recalling the definition of
\|\cdot\|_{\widetilde{\psi}_{\frac{1}{2}}}
from Lemma
39
,

\displaystyle\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\phi_{j}^{f_{k,i}}-\bar{\phi}_{j}^{f_{k,\cdot}})^{2}\Big\|_{\widetilde{\Psi}_{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\phi_{j}^{f_{k,i}})^{2}\Big]-n(\bar{\phi}_{j}^{f_{k,\cdot}})^{2}\Big\|_{\widetilde{\Psi}_{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq n\max_{i=1}^{n}\|(\phi_{j}^{f_{k,i}})^{2}\|_{\widetilde{\Psi}_{\frac{1}{2}}}+\Big\|(\sqrt{n}\bar{\phi}_{j}^{f_{k,\cdot}})^{2}\Big\|_{\widetilde{\Psi}_{\frac{1}{2}}}

(Lemma
40
)

\displaystyle\leq Cn\max_{i=1}^{n}\|\phi_{j}^{f_{k,i}}\|_{\Psi_{1}}^{2}+C\|\sqrt{n}\bar{\phi}_{j}^{f_{k,\cdot}}\|_{\Psi_{1}}^{2}

(Lemma
39
, Lemma
42
(i))

\displaystyle\leq Cn\max_{i=1}^{n}\|\phi_{j}^{f_{k,i}}\|_{\Psi_{1}}^{2}+C\max_{i=1}^{n}\|\phi_{j}^{f_{k,i}}\|_{\Psi_{1}}^{2}

(Lemma
41
(iii))

\displaystyle\leq nC\max_{i=1}^{n}\|\phi_{j}^{f_{k,i}}\|_{\Psi_{1}}^{2},

where the value of the constant
C
changed from line to line. Therefore, we find by Lemma
39

\Big\|\|T^{e}-T^{e,M}\|_{\Psi_{1}|\sigma(f)}\Big\|_{\widetilde{\Psi}_{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq C\log^{3}(K+1)\max_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{j=M+1}^{\infty}\max_{i=1}^{n}\|\phi_{i}^{k,j}\|_{\Psi_{1}}^{2}.

Let
R_{2}(M):=\sum_{j=M+1}^{\infty}C_{\phi}^{2}j^{-2\alpha}
. By Assumption (A2) stated in Assumption
1
,

\Big\|\|T^{e}-T^{e,M}\|_{\Psi_{1}|\sigma(f)}\Big\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq C\log^{3}(K+1)R_{2}(M)\lesssim\frac{C_{\phi}^{2}}{2\alpha-1}\log^{3}(K+1)M^{1-2\alpha}.

\Box

S.6.10
Results related to stochastic processes

The random elements defined in
1
are also measurable stochastic processes:

Lemma 27
.

Suppose
f
is a Borel measurable random element of the space
(C[I])^{K}
, defined on a measure space
\Omega
. Then
X(\omega,t):=f(\omega)(t)
is a measurable
K
-dimensional stochastic process defined on
\Omega\times I
.

The following result must be known to the community, but we were unable to find a reference and so provide a proof for completeness.

Lemma 28
.

Let
f=\oplus_{k=1}^{K}f_{k}
be a
K
dimensional stochastic process with index set
I
. Suppose for all
1\leq k\leq K
the real valued process
f_{k}
is centered, second order, and mean-square continuous, so that it satisfies the hypotheses of the Karhunen–Loève Theorem (Theorem
6
). Then,

1.

There exists a
K
dimensional Gaussian process
G
with real valued marginals
G_{1},\dots,G_{K}
such that for all
1\leq k_{1}\leq k_{2}\leq K
and all
t_{1},t_{2}\in I
,

\Cov\Big(G_{k_{1}}(t_{1}),G_{k_{2}}(t_{2})\Big)=\Cov\Big(f_{k_{1}}(t_{1}),f_{k_{2}}(t_{2})\Big).

2.

For
1\leq k\leq K
let
\{\zeta^{k}_{1},\zeta^{k}_{2},\dots\}
be the Karhunen–Loève scores of
G_{k}
, and let
\{\phi^{f_{k}}_{1},\phi^{f_{k}}_{2},\dots\}
be the Karhunen–Loève scores of
f_{k}
. For any finite collection of natural numbers
\{n_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
,
\oplus_{k=1}^{K}\oplus_{i=1}^{N}\zeta^{k}_{n_{i}}
is a Gaussian random vector, and
\Var(\oplus_{k=1}^{K}\oplus_{i=1}^{N}\zeta^{k}_{n_{i}})=\Var(\oplus_{k=1}^{K}\oplus_{i=1}^{N}\phi^{f_{k}}_{n_{i}})
.

We recall some classical results which we state here for the reader’s convenience.

Theorem 5
.

(Mercer’s Theorem)
Let
K(s,t)
be a continuous symmetric and non-negative definite function defined on
I\times I\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Consider the linear operator
\mathcal{K}
defined for each
f\in L^{2}(I)
by
\mathcal{K}(f)=\int_{I}K(s,\cdot)f(s)ds
. Each eigen-value of
\mathcal{K}
,
\lambda_{j}
, is real and non-negative, and the corresponding eigen-vectors can be chosen to be an orthonormal set. Further, if
(\lambda_{j},e_{j})
are the eigen-value and eigen-vector pairs derived from
\mathcal{K}
,

K(s,t)=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}e_{j}(s)e_{j}(t),

for all
s,t
, with the sum converging absolutely and uniformly.

For a proof, observe that the operator
\mathcal{K}
is compact by Theorem 4.6.2 in
Hsing and
Eubank 2015
, and this implies that the pairs
(\lambda_{j},e_{j})
exist. The decomposition of
K
follows from Theorem 4.6.5 in
Hsing and
Eubank 2015
. The following result follows from Theorem 7.3.5 in
Hsing and
Eubank 2015
and its proof.

Theorem 6
.

(Karhunen–Loève Theorem)
Let
I
be compact, let
\Omega
be a probability space, and let
X(\omega,t):\Omega\times I\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be an integrable stochastic process. Suppose that

1.

(centered, second order) For all
s,t\in I
,
\mathbb{E}[X_{t}]=0
and
Cov(X_{t},X_{s})<\infty
.

2.

(mean square continuous) If
t_{n}\rightarrow t
, then
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}[(X_{t_{n}}-X_{t})^{2}]=0
.

Let
K(s,t)=\Cov(X_{s},X_{t})
. For each
j
, let
Z_{j}\in L^{2}(I)
be the eigen-vector of
\mathcal{K}
corresponding to the
j^{th}
eigen-value
\lambda_{j}
of
\mathcal{K}
(where this operator is defined in the statement of Theorem
5
), so that the functions
Z_{j}
are orthonormal in
L^{2}[0,1]
. Further, let
\phi_{j}=\int_{I}Z_{j}(t)X_{t}
. Then the random variables
\phi_{j}
are pair-wise uncorrelated, satisfy
\Var(\phi_{j})=\lambda_{j}
, and for all
M
,

\sup_{t\in I}\mathbb{E}[|X_{t}-\sum_{j=1}^{M}\phi_{j}Z_{j}(t)|^{2}]=\sup_{t\in I}[K(t,t)-\sum_{j=1}^{M}\lambda_{j}e_{j}^{2}(t)]

Furthermore,

\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{t\in I}[K(t,t)-\sum_{j=1}^{M}\lambda_{j}e_{j}^{2}(t)]=0.

Lemma 29
.

Let
f_{t}
be a stochastic process that satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
6
, and recall the notation introduced in the statement of that theorem. Let
K
denote the covariance kernel of
f_{t}
.
Then,
\mathbb{E}[\int f_{t}^{2}dt]<\infty
. Moreover, for all
M\geq 0
,
R_{M+1}:=\sum_{j=M+1}^{\infty}\phi_{j}^{2}
is almost surely finite,

(i)0\leq\int_{I}f_{t}^{2}dt-\sum_{j=1}^{M}\phi_{j}^{2}=R_{M+1},

and

(ii)\|R_{M+1}\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\leq C_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}\sum_{j=M+1}^{\infty}\|\phi_{j}^{2}\|_{\Psi_{\frac{1}{2}}},

where the right-hand side is defined to be
+\infty
if for any
j\geq M+1
,
\|\phi_{j}\|_{\psi_{\frac{1}{2}}}=\infty
(recall Definition
4
.)

The next few results are elementary and are included for the reader’s convenience:

Definition 3
.

Let
d
be a metric on
I
, and let
\{f_{t};t\in I\}
be a real-valued stochastic process. If for each
(s,t)\in I^{2}
it holds that
\|f_{s}-f_{t}\|_{\Psi_{1}}\leq d(s,t)
, we say that
f\in\textrm{subExp}(d)
.

The following proof is elementary and can be found in
Decker 2024
(see Lemma E.0.9).

Lemma 30
.

Let
0<q<1
. Then function
d_{q}(s,t):=|s-t|^{q}
is a metric on
\mathbb{R}.

We next recall that sub-exponentiality implies a.s. continuity of sample paths, see Exercise 17 on p. 106 in
Van Der Vaart and
Wellner 2023
.

Lemma 31
.

Let
0<q\leq 1
,
C_{H}>0
be real constants and let
d_{q}(x,y)=|x-y|^{q}
(by Lemma
30
it is a metric). If
f\in\textrm{subExp}(C_{H}d_{q})
then almost every sample path of
f
is a continuous function on
I
.

Lemma 32
.

Let
0<q\leq 1
,
C_{H}>0
, and recall that
d_{q}(s,t)=|s-t|^{q}
is a metric (Lemma
30
). There is a constant
K_{*}
that does not depend on
C_{H}
or
q
such that for all
f\in\textrm{subExp}(C_{H}d_{q})
,

\Big\|\sup_{(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})\in I^{2}}\{|f_{\theta_{1}}-f_{\theta_{2}}|\}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}\leq K_{*}\frac{C_{H}}{q}.

(148)

Lemma 33
.

Let
0<q\leq 1
,
C_{H}>0
, and recall that
d_{q}(s,t)=|s-t|^{q}
is a metric (Lemma
30
).
Then for the constant
K_{*}
from Lemma
32
, for all
f\in\textrm{subExp}(C_{H}d_{q})
there

\Big\|\sup_{t\in I}f_{t}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}\leq\frac{K_{*}C_{H}}{q}+\Bigg\|\sqrt{\int_{I}f_{t}^{2}dt}\Bigg\|_{\Psi_{1}}

(149)

Proof of Lemma
27

The random element
f
is assumed to be a measurable map from
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
into
((C[I])^{K},\mathcal{B})
, where
\mathcal{B}
is the Borel sigma algebra generated by the canonical topology (i.e. the topology induced by the norm
f\rightarrow\max_{k=1}^{K}\sup_{t\in I}|\pi_{k}f(t)|
.) We must show that
X(\omega,t):=f(\omega)(t)
is a jointly measurable map defined on
\Omega\times I
and taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{K}
. It suffices that for almost every
\omega
the map
t\rightarrow X(\omega,t):I\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K}
is continuous with respect to the Euclidean norm and also that for each
t
the map
\omega\rightarrow X(\omega,t):\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K}
is measurable. These two separate conditions imply that
X(\omega,t)
is jointly measurable by Lemma 4.51 of
Guide 2006
.

First, note that for fixed
\omega
,
t\rightarrow X(\omega,t)=t\rightarrow\oplus_{k=1}^{K}\pi_{k}[f(\omega)(t)]
, and we have assumed that for every
\omega
it holds for every
k
that
\pi_{k}[f(\omega)(\cdot)]\in C[I]
. Continuity of each component of a map into
K
dimensional Euclidean space implies continuity of the map.

Second, note that for each fixed
t
,
\omega\rightarrow X(\omega,t)=e_{t}\circ f(\omega)
, where
e_{t}:(C[I])^{K}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{K}
is defined by
e_{t}(f)=f(t)
. It is a well known result of point set topology that
e_{t}
is continuous with respect to the canonical supremum topology. Since
f
is assumed to be measureable, the composition is measureable.
\Box

Proof of Lemma
28

Recall that
X
is a
K
dimensional Gaussian process if and only if
X
is a measureable
\mathbb{R}^{K}
valued stochastic process and for every finite collection
\{t_{1},\cdots,t_{L}\}\subset I
the vector
\oplus_{\ell=1}^{L}\oplus_{k=1}^{K}X_{k}(t_{\ell})\in(\mathbb{R}^{K})^{L}
has a multivariate normal distribution. We will construct a Gaussian process
G
with the properties indicated in (a) by invoking the Kolmogorov Extension Theorem (see
Oksendal 2013
, Theorem 2.1.5).

Given a collection
t_{1},\dots t_{L}\subset I
, let
\Sigma_{t_{1},\dots t_{L}}
be the
(KL)\times(KL)
positive semi-definite matrix defined by
\Sigma_{t_{1},\dots t_{L}}:=\Var(\oplus_{l=1}^{L}\oplus_{k=1}^{K}f_{k}(t_{l}))
. Let
g_{t_{1},\dots t_{L}}
be a centered Gaussian vector with covariance matrix
\Sigma_{t_{1},\dots t_{L}}
, and let
\nu_{t_{1},\dots t_{L}}
be the measure on
(\mathbb{R}^{K})^{L}
that is defined on each Borel
F\subset(\mathbb{R}^{K})^{L}
by
\nu_{t_{1},\dots t_{L}}(F)={\mathrm{P}}(g_{t_{1},\dots t_{L}}\in F)
.

Let
e_{1},\dots,e_{KL}
be the standard basis of
(\mathbb{R}^{K})^{L}
. To facilitate computations, we use an alternate labeling of the basis vectors. For
1\leq l\leq L
, and
1\leq k\leq K
, let
f_{l,k}={e_{(l-1)L+k}}
. Let
\sigma
be a permutation of
\{1,\dots,L\}
, and define the invertible linear operator
L_{\sigma}:(\mathbb{R}^{K})^{L}\rightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{K})^{L}
through its action on each basis vector
f_{l,k}
:

L_{\sigma}(f_{l,k})=f_{\sigma(l),k}.

(150)

It is straightforward to see that if
F_{1},F_{2},\dots F_{L}
are Borel sets in
\mathbb{R}^{K}
,

L_{\sigma}(F_{1}\times F_{2}\times\dots F_{L})=F_{\sigma(1)}\times F_{\sigma(2)}\times\dots F_{\sigma(L)}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\nu_{t_{1},\dots t_{L}}(F_{1}\times F_{2}\times\dots F_{L})

\displaystyle={\mathrm{P}}(g_{t_{1},\dots t_{L}}\in F_{1}\times F_{2}\times\dots F_{L})

\displaystyle={\mathrm{P}}(L_{\sigma}g_{t_{1},\dots t_{L}}\in F_{\sigma(1)}\times F_{\sigma(2)}\times\dots F_{\sigma(L)})

\displaystyle={\mathrm{P}}(Y\in F_{\sigma(1)}\times F_{\sigma(2)}\times\dots F_{\sigma(L)}),

for
Y
a centered gaussian with covariance matrix
L_{\sigma}\Big(\Sigma_{t_{1},\dots t_{L}}\Big)L_{\sigma}^{\top}
. Since

\displaystyle L_{\sigma}\Big(\Sigma_{t_{1},\dots t_{L}}\Big)L_{\sigma}^{\top}=L_{\sigma}\Var(\oplus_{l=1}^{L}\oplus_{k=1}^{K}f_{k}(t_{l}))L_{\sigma}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\Var(L_{\sigma}\oplus_{l=1}^{L}\oplus_{k=1}^{K}f_{k}(t_{l}))

\displaystyle=\Var(\oplus_{l=1}^{L}\oplus_{k=1}^{K}f_{k}(t_{\sigma(l)}))

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{t_{\sigma(1)},\dots t_{\sigma(L)}},

it follows that

\displaystyle\nu_{t_{1},\dots t_{L}}(F_{1}\times F_{2}\times\dots\times F_{L})={\mathrm{P}}(Y\in F_{\sigma(1)}\times F_{\sigma(2)}\times\dots\times F_{\sigma(L)})

\displaystyle={\mathrm{P}}(g_{t_{\sigma(1)},\dots,\sigma(L)}\in F_{\sigma(1)}\times F_{\sigma(2)}\times\dots\times F_{\sigma(L)})

\displaystyle=\nu_{t_{\sigma(1)},\dots,\sigma(L)}(F_{\sigma(1)}\times F_{\sigma(2)}\times\dots\times F_{\sigma(L)}).

Thus the family of jointly gaussian measures
\nu_{t_{1},\dots t_{L}}
satisfies the first consistency condition in the Kolomogorov Extension Theorem. The second consistency condition holds trivially. Then by the Kolmogorov Extension Theorem there exists a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},P)
and a stochastic process
G_{t}=\oplus_{k=1}^{K}G_{k}(t)
on
\Omega
, such that for every finite sub-collection
\{t_{1},\dots,t_{L}\}

\oplus_{\ell=1}^{T}\oplus_{k=1}^{K}G_{k}(t_{\ell})
is a Gausssian random vector with covariance matrix
\Var(\oplus_{l=1}^{L}\oplus_{k=1}^{K}f_{k}(t_{l}))
. This implies in particular that for all
1\leq k_{1}\leq k_{2}\leq K
and all
t_{1},t_{2}\in I
,
\Cov(G_{k_{1}}(t_{1}),G_{k_{2}}(t_{2}))=\Cov(f_{k_{1}}(t_{1}),f_{k_{2}}(t_{2}))
.

To prove part (b), recall that we assumed that for each
1\leq k\leq K
, the stochastic process
f_{k}
is centered, second order, and mean square continuous. These conditions imply that
(s,t)\mapsto\Cov(f_{k}(s),f_{k}(t))=\Cov(G_{k}(s),G_{k}(t))
is a continuous function defined on
I\times I
. Let
Z^{f_{k}}_{j}
denote the eigenfunctions of
(s,t)\mapsto\Cov(f_{k}(s),f_{k}(t))
. By Theorem
6
the corresponding KL scores are given by
\phi^{f_{k}}_{j}=\int_{I}Z^{f_{k}}_{j}(t)f_{k}(t)dt
. Since
\Cov(f_{k}(s),f_{k}(t))=\Cov(G_{k}(s),G_{k}(t))
for all
s,t,k
, the covariance operator of
G_{k}
has the same eingenfunctions and eigenvalues. The corresponding KL scores satisfy
\zeta^{k}_{j}=\int_{I}Z^{f_{k}}_{j}(t)f_{k}(t)dt
by Theorem
6
.

If
Z^{f_{k}}_{j}
corresponds to a zero eigenvalue, then almost surely
\phi^{f_{k}}_{j}=\zeta^{k}_{j}=0
, since
f_{k}
and
G_{k}
concentrate in sub-space that is orthogonal to
Z^{f_{k}}_{j}
as discussed on page 182 of
Hsing and
Eubank 2015
. Hence, in what follows it suffices to prove joint gaussianity of the collections
(\zeta^{k}_{j})_{(k,j)\in S}
for finite sets
S\subset\{1,\dots,K\}\times\mathbb{N}
such that each
Z^{f_{k}}_{j}
corresponds to a non-zero eigenvalues.

Each such
Z^{f_{k}}_{j}
is continuous. To see this, denote by
\lambda
the corresponding eigenvalue and note that

\lambda(Z(t)-Z(t^{\prime}))=\int_{I}Z(s)(K(s,t)-K(s^{\prime},t))ds\to 0\quad\mbox{as }t\to t^{\prime}

by continuity and boundedness of the covariance kernel
K
combined with square integrability of
Z
and the dominated convergence theorem. By Theorem
6
,

\displaystyle\Cov(\phi^{f^{k_{1}}}_{j_{1}},\phi^{f^{k_{2}}}_{j_{2}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\int_{I}f_{k_{1}}(s)Z^{f_{k_{1}}}_{j_{1}}(s)ds\Big)\Big(\int_{I}f_{k_{2}}(t)Z^{f_{k_{2}}}_{j_{2}}(t)dt\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\int_{I}\int_{I}\mathbb{E}[f_{k_{1}}(s)f_{k_{2}}(t)]Z^{f_{k_{1}}}_{j_{1}}(s)Z^{f_{k_{2}}}_{j_{2}}(t)dsdt

\displaystyle=\int_{I}\int_{I}\Cov(f_{k_{1}}(s),f_{k_{2}}(t))Z^{f_{k_{1}}}_{j_{1}}(s)Z^{f_{k_{2}}}_{j_{2}}(t)dsdt

\displaystyle=\int_{I}\int_{I}\Cov(G_{k_{1}}(s),G_{k_{2}}(t))Z^{G_{k_{1}}}_{j_{1}}(s)Z^{G_{k_{2}}}_{j_{2}}(t)dsdt

\displaystyle=\Cov(\zeta^{k_{1}}_{j_{1}},\zeta^{k_{2}}_{j_{2}}),

where we used Fubini’s Theorem to justify interchange of the expectation and integral, and the result of part (a) in the second last equality. Further, interpreting
(\zeta^{k}_{j})_{(k,j)\in S}
as vector in
\mathbb{R}^{S}

\displaystyle(\zeta^{k}_{j})_{(k,j)\in S}=\Big(\int_{I}G_{k}(t)Z^{f_{k}}_{j}(t)dt\Big)_{(k,j)\in S}=\Big(\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}M^{-1}\sum_{m=1}^{M}G_{k}(m/M)Z^{f_{k}}_{j}(m/M)\Big)_{(k,j)\in S}

\displaystyle=\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\Big(M^{-1}\sum_{m=1}^{M}G_{k}(m/M)Z^{f_{k}}_{j}(m/M)\Big)_{(k,j)\in S}=\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\Big(M^{-1}G_{k}(m/M)Z^{f_{k}}_{j}(m/M)\Big)_{(k,j)\in S}.

We have used that each
Z^{f_{k}k}_{j}
is a continuous function, and that
G_{k}
has an almost surely uniformly continuous modification; it satisfies
\|G_{k}(s)-G_{k}(t)\|_{\Psi_{1}}\lesssim\sqrt{\Var(G_{k}(s)-G_{k}(t))}=\sqrt{\Var(f_{k}(s)-f_{k}(t))}\lesssim\|f_{k}(s)-f_{k}(t))\|_{\Psi_{1}}\leq C_{H}|s-t|^{q}

and then we can apply the conclusion of Exercise 17, page 106 of
Van Der Vaart and
Wellner 2023
Therefore the product
G_{k}Z_{j}^{k}
is continuous, and so Riemann integrable, and we may therefore apply Lemma
44
, which justifies the above display.

Note that
\oplus_{m=1}^{M}\big(M^{-1}G_{k}(m/M)Z^{k}_{j}(m/M)\Big)_{(k,j)\in S}
is a multi-variate centered gaussian because
G
is a gaussian process. Thus for each
M
,
\sum_{m=1}^{M}\big(M^{-1}G_{k}(m/M)Z^{f_{k}}_{j}(m/M)\big)_{(k,j)\in S}
is a centered Gaussian. The sequence converges almost surely by Lemma
44
, hence converges in distribution. It is well known that the distributional limit, when it exists, of a sequence of Gaussians is gaussian.

\Box

Proof of Lemma
29

For a second order and mean-square continuous process
g_{t}
with
\mathbb{E}[g_{t}^{2}]<\infty
, Fubini’s Theorem gives
\int_{I\times\Omega}g(\omega,t)d(\mu\times\omega)=\mathbb{E}[\int_{I}g_{t}d\mu_{t}]=\int_{I}\mathbb{E}[g_{t}]d\mu_{t}
, here
\mu
corresponds to Lebesgue measure on
I=[0,1]
. Apply this with
g_{t}=f_{t}
to conclude that
\mathbb{E}\big[\int_{I}f_{t}^{2}dt\big]<\infty
. This implies that
\int_{I}f_{t}^{2}dt
is almost surely finite. Next, note that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\int_{I}\Big(f_{t}-\sum_{j=1}^{M}\phi_{j}Z_{j}(t)\Big)^{2}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{I}f_{t}^{2}dt+\int_{I}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{M}\phi_{j}Z_{j}(t)\Big)^{2}dt-2\Big(\int_{I}f_{t}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\phi_{j}Z_{j}(t)\Big)dt

\displaystyle=\int_{I}f_{t}^{2}dt-\sum_{j=1}^{M}\phi_{j}^{2},

where the last equality follows by Theorem
6
, using the fact that
\int_{I}Z_{j}(t)Z_{k}(t)dt=\delta_{kj}
and
\int_{I}f(t)Z_{j}(t)dt=\phi_{j}
. Let
S_{M}=\sum_{j=1}^{M}\phi_{j}^{2}
, and note that by the bound just proved and since
S_{M}
is non-decreasing,
S_{M}
converges point-wise to an almost surely finite limit. Note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big|\sum_{j=1}^{M}\phi_{j}^{2}-\int_{I}f_{t}^{2}dt\Big|

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{I}\Big(f_{t}-\sum_{j=1}^{M}\phi_{j}Z_{j}(t)\Big)^{2}dt\Big]

\displaystyle=\int_{I}\mathbb{E}\Big(f_{t}-\sum_{j=1}^{M}\phi_{j}Z_{j}(t)\Big)^{2}dt

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\in I}\mathbb{E}\Big(f_{t}-\sum_{j=1}^{M}\phi_{j}Z_{j}(t)\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t\in I}\Big|K(t,t)-\sum_{j=1}^{M}\lambda_{j}Z_{j}^{2}(t)\Big|

where the first equality was established in the beginning of this proof, the second equality follows from Fubini’s Theorem, and the last inequality is from Theorem
6
, which also shows the the final term tends to zero as
M
goes to
\infty
. It follows that
S_{M}
converges almost surely to
\int_{I}f_{t}^{2}dt
. Therefore for any
M\geq 0
(in case
M=0
the sum
\sum_{j=1}^{M}\phi_{j}^{2}
is defined to be
0
) ,

0\leq\int_{I}f_{t}^{2}dt-\sum_{j=1}^{M}\phi_{j}^{2}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\big(S_{N}-\sum_{j=1}^{M}\phi_{j}^{2}\big)=R_{M+1}\leq\int_{I}f_{t}^{2}dt,

and this shows that
R_{M+1}
is almost surely finite and also (i).
To complete the proof of (ii), apply Lemma
40
with
p=1/2
, setting
X_{j}
in the statement of the Lemma as
X_{j}=\phi_{j+M}^{2}
. Note that we may assume that
\sum_{j=M+1}^{\infty}\|\phi_{j}^{2}\|_{\psi_{1/2}}<\infty
since otherwise the bound holds trivially.

\Box

Proof of Lemma
32

By Corollary 2.2.5 in
Van Der Vaart and
Wellner 2023
, there exists a constant
C
depending only on
C_{H}
such that

\Big\|\sup_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}}|f_{\theta_{1}}-f_{\theta_{2}}|\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}\leq C\int_{0}^{1}\Psi_{1}^{-1}(D(\varepsilon,d_{q}))d\varepsilon.

(151)

Inspection of the proof of Theorem 2.2.4 in
Van Der Vaart and
Wellner 2023
shows that
C\leq C_{1}C_{H}
for a universal constant
C_{1}
. Recall that
\Psi_{1}^{-1}(x)=\log(x+1)
. By elementary computations for every
1\geq\varepsilon>0
,
D(\varepsilon,d_{q},[0,1])\leq\frac{2^{1/q}}{2}\varepsilon^{-1/q}
. Thus, using
x\geq 1\rightarrow\log(x+1)\leq\log(2x)
,

\displaystyle\Big\|\sup_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}}\{|f_{\theta_{1}}-f_{\theta_{2}}|\}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}\leq C\Big[\int_{0}^{1}\log\Big(\frac{2^{1/q}}{2}\varepsilon^{-1/q}+1\Big)d\varepsilon\Big]

\displaystyle\leq C\Big[\frac{1}{q}\int_{0}^{1}\log\Big(\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\Big)d\varepsilon\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{C}{q}\Big(\log(2)-\int_{0}^{1}\log(\varepsilon)d\varepsilon\Big)=\frac{C}{q}\Big(\log(2)-[\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0^{+}}\varepsilon\log(\varepsilon)-\varepsilon]|^{1}_{\delta}\Big)=\frac{C}{q}(\log(2)+1)\leq\frac{2C_{1}C_{H}}{q}.

Let
K_{*}:=2C_{1}
.
\Box

Proof of Lemma
33
We have

\displaystyle|f_{t}|

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{s\in[0,1]}\Big\{|f_{t}-f_{s}|+|f_{s}|\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{s\in I}\Big\{\sup_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in I}|f_{\theta_{1}}-f_{\theta_{2}}|+|f_{s}|\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in I}\Big\{|f_{\theta_{1}}-f_{\theta_{2}}|\Big\}+\inf_{s\in I}|f_{s}|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in I}\Big\{|f_{\theta_{1}}-f_{\theta_{2}}|\Big\}+\int_{0}^{1}|f_{s}|ds

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in I}\Big\{|f_{\theta_{1}}-f_{\theta_{2}}|\Big\}+\sqrt{\int_{0}^{1}f_{s}^{2}ds}.

By monotonicity, i.e.
0\leq X\leq Y
a.s. implies
\|X\|_{\Psi_{1}}\leq\|Y\|_{\Psi_{1}}
, and the triangle inequality,

\Big\|\sup_{t}|f_{t}|\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}\leq\Big\|\sup_{\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in I}\{|f_{\theta_{1}}-f_{\theta_{2}}|\}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}+\Big\|\sqrt{\int_{0}^{1}f_{s}^{2}}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}.

(152)

The claim follows from Lemma
32
.
\Box

S.6.11
Miscellaneous Technical Results

The following bound is standard:

Lemma 34
.

For
M\geq 1
and
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}

\sum_{j=M+1}^{\infty}j^{-2\alpha}\leq\int_{M}^{\infty}x^{-2\alpha}dx=\frac{M^{1-2\alpha}}{2\alpha-1}.

Further,

\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}j^{-2\alpha}\leq\Big(1+\int_{1}^{\infty}x^{-2\alpha}dx\Big)=\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha-1}.

Lemma 35
.

Assume that (A2) holds. The random variable
T^{M,G}
defined in section
S.4
is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure. Furthermore under the conditions of
1
, there is a universal constant
C
such that for all
M\geq 1
,

\|T^{M,G}\|_{\Psi_{1}}\leq C\log(K+1)C_{\phi}^{2}\frac{2\alpha}{2\alpha-1}.

(153)

This implies that for each
t\geq 0
,

{\mathrm{P}}(T^{M,G}>t)\leq 2\exp\Big(-\frac{2\alpha-1}{{2\alpha}C{C_{\phi}^{2}}}\cdot\frac{t}{\log(K+1)}\Big).

(154)

Further, for each
\epsilon>0
,

{\mathrm{P}}\Big(T^{M,G}\geq\log(K+1)\log\big(\tfrac{2}{\varepsilon}\big)\frac{2\alpha C{C_{\phi}^{2}}}{2\alpha-1}\Big)\leq\varepsilon.

(155)

Proof of Lemma
35

Let
E
be a null set, and recall that
T^{M,G}=\max_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{M}g_{k,j}^{2}
, where
\oplus_{k=1}^{K}\oplus_{j=1}^{M}g_{k,j}
is Gaussian and
\Var(g_{k,j})\leq C_{1,2}^{2}C_{\phi}^{2}j^{-2\alpha}
where
C_{1,2}
is the constant from Lemma
42
. We have

{\mathrm{P}}(T^{M,G}\in E)={\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{M}g_{jk}^{2}\in E\Big)\leq{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\exists k:\sum_{j=1}^{M}g_{jk}^{2}\in E\Big){\leq K\max_{k=1}^{K}{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{M}g_{jk}^{2}\in E\Big)}

by the union bound.

By construction, for fixed
k
,
g_{jk}
are uncorrelated and Gaussian. Each
g_{jk}^{2}
is absolutely continuous, being equal in distribution to a scaled
\chi^{2}(1)
random variable. Convolution preserves absolutely continuity. The norm bound, follows from (A2) by routine calculations after noting that chi-square random variables have finite sub-Gaussian norms and applying Lemma
41
(ii) and Lemma
34
. The tail bound follows from Lemma
41
(iv) and the final bound by rearranging the tail bound.

\Box

Lemma 36
(A Kolmogorov Bound implies a bound on quantiles).

Let
F
and
G
be cumulative distribution functions, and let
q_{F}
and
q_{G}
be their respective quantile functions. Let
t_{*}\in\mathbb{R},\delta>0
, and suppose

\sup_{t\geq t_{*}}|F(t)-G(t)|\leq\delta.

(156)

If
q_{F}(\alpha)>t_{*}
then

q_{G}(\alpha-\delta)\leq q_{F}(\alpha)\leq q_{G}(\alpha+\delta).

Further,
G(t_{*})+\delta<\alpha
implies
q_{F}(\alpha)>t_{*}
.

Proof of Lemma
36

Let
H:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a non-decreasing right continuous function, and recall that
q_{H}(\alpha):=\inf A_{H}(\alpha)
, where

A_{H}(\alpha):=\{t:H(t)\geq\alpha\},

and
\inf\varnothing=\infty
by convention. We will first prove that
G(t_{*})+\delta<\alpha
implies
q_{F}(\alpha)>t_{*}
and then complete the proof assuming the latter. By assumption
G(t_{*})+\delta<\alpha
implies
F(t_{*})\leq G(t_{*})+\delta<\alpha
. By right-continuity of
F
there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
F(t_{*}+\varepsilon)<\alpha
and thus by definition
q_{F}(\alpha)\geq t_{*}+\varepsilon>t_{*}.
From now on assume
q_{F}(\alpha)>t_{*}
.

Assume that
x\in A_{F}(\alpha)
. Then
F(x)\geq\alpha
and

t_{*}<q_{F}(\alpha)=\inf A_{F}(\alpha)
implies
t_{*}<x
. Since
t_{*}<x
, (
156
) implies
G(x)+\delta\geq F(x)\geq\alpha
where the last bound follows from
x\in A_{F}(\alpha)

This inequality means that
x\in A_{G+\delta}(\alpha)
. Hence
A_{F}(\alpha)\subseteq A_{G+\delta}(\alpha)
. Therefore,

q_{F}(\alpha)=\inf A_{F}(\alpha)\geq\inf A_{G+\delta}(\alpha)=\inf A_{G}(\alpha-\delta)=q_{G}(\alpha-\delta).

Likewise,
x\in A_{F}(\alpha)
implies
G(x)-\delta\leq F(x)
so that
A_{G-\delta}(\alpha)\subseteq A_{F}(\alpha)
, and therefore,

q_{F}(\alpha)=\inf A_{F}(\alpha)\leq\inf A_{G-\delta}(\alpha)=\inf A_{G}(\alpha+\delta)=q_{G}(\alpha+\delta).

Combining the two inequalities yields the result.
\Box

Lemma 37
.

Let
X\in(\mathbb{R}^{M})^{K}
be a centered Gaussian vector with positive semi definite covariance matrix
\Sigma
. Let
\Sigma_{k}:=\Var(\pi_{k}X)
, and suppose
\max_{k=1}^{K}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{M}[\Sigma_{k}]_{jj}}\leq U
. Then for all
0<\varepsilon\leq 1
,

\Big\|\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}X\|_{2}\Big\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq K_{\Psi_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}\log^{\frac{1}{2}}(K+1)U,

and

{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}X\|_{2}\geq\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}K_{\Psi_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\log(K+1)}U\sqrt{\log\big(\tfrac{2}{\varepsilon}\big)}\Big)\leq\varepsilon,

where
K_{\Psi_{1}}
is the universal constant of Lemma
41
(ii).

Proof of Lemma
37

We have

\displaystyle\Big\|\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}X\|_{2}\Big\|_{\psi_{2}}=\Big(\Big\|\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}X\|^{2}_{2}\Big\|_{\Psi_{1}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

Lemma
42
(i)

\displaystyle\leq K_{\Psi_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\log^{\frac{1}{2}}(K+1)\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\|[\pi_{k}X]^{2}_{j}\|_{\Psi_{1}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

Lemma
41
(ii)

\displaystyle\leq K_{\Psi_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\log^{\frac{1}{2}}(K+1)\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\|[\pi_{k}X]_{j}\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Lemma
42
(i)

Since
[\pi_{k}X]_{j}
is a centered Gaussian,
\|[\pi_{k}X]_{j}\|_{\psi_{2}}=\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}\sqrt{\Var([\pi_{k}X]_{j})}=\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}[\Sigma_{k}]_{jj}}
(this follows by homogeneity of the norm; the exact constant follows by calculating
\|N(0,1)\|_{\psi_{2}}
from the definition). Thus,

\Big\|\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}X\Big\|_{2}\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq K_{\Psi_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}\log^{\frac{1}{2}}(K+1)U.

By Lemma
41
(iv),for any
t
,

{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\max_{k=1}^{K}\|\pi_{k}X\|_{2}\geq t\Big)\leq 2\exp\Bigg(-\Bigg(\frac{t}{K_{\Psi_{1}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}\log^{\frac{1}{2}}(K+1)U}\Bigg)^{2}\Bigg).

Rearranging the bound yields the result.
\Box

S.6.12
Useful Properties of Orlicz spaces

The material in this section is known in the community, but scattered. We provide a collection of the results we will use along with precise statements for the reader’s convenience. We also provide short proofs for results we could not find in the literature in the exact form we need them. Recall the following definitions, see also Section 2.7.1 in
Vershynin 2018
and Section 2.2.1 in
Van Der Vaart and
Wellner 2023
. If
X
is a topological space, we define
\mathcal{B}_{X}
to be the generated Borel
\sigma
-field on
X
.

Definition 4
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space. If
X:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{R}}/\mathcal{F}
measureable and
\psi
is a measureable scalar function,

\|X\|_{\psi}:=\inf\Big\{B>0:\mathbb{E}\psi\big(\tfrac{|X|}{B}\big)\leq 1\Big\},

(157)

with the convention that
\inf\varnothing=\infty
. We then define

L^{\psi}=\{X,\|X\|_{\psi}<\infty\}.

(158)

A non-zero function
\psi:[0,\infty)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
is an Orlicz function if and only if
\psi
is convex, non-decreasing,
\psi(0)=0
and
\psi
is not identically zero.

Lemma 38
.

If
\psi
is an Orlicz function,
L^{\psi}
is a Banach space.

This is mentioned in Section 2.7.1 of
Vershynin 2018
, see
Decker 2024
for a proof (see Lemma H.0.1). Of special interest in this article are the functions
\psi_{p}
,

\psi_{p}(x):=\exp(|x|^{p})-1.

If
p\geq 1
,
\psi_{p}
is an Orlicz function. Otherwise,
\|\cdot\|_{\psi_{p}}
is only a quasi-norm (the triangle inequality fails). Nonetheless we have:

Lemma 39
(The
\psi_{p}
quasi-norm is equivalent to an Orlicz norm).

If
1\leq p
,
\psi_{p}
is an Orlicz function. If
0<p
, there exists an Orlicz function
\widetilde{\psi}_{p}
such that
\|X\|_{\widetilde{\psi}_{p}}\leq\|X\|_{{\psi}_{p}}\leq C_{\psi_{p}}\|X\|_{\widetilde{\psi}_{p}}
, for
C_{\psi_{p}}:=e^{-1}[\frac{e}{\log(2)p}]^{\frac{1}{p}}.

This result can be found in Exercise 2.14.1 in
Van Der Vaart and
Wellner 2023
, the exact form of the constants
C_{\psi_{p}}
is worked out it
Decker 2024
(see Lemma H.0.2). This equivalence implies that the triangle inequality holds up to a constant for infinite sums in
L^{\psi_{p}}
.

Lemma 40
(Triangle inequality for infinite sums, up to constant).

Whenever
p>0
, if
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\|X_{j}\|_{\psi_{p}}<\infty
, there exists
L\in L^{\psi_{p}}
such that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\Big\|\sum_{j=1}^{N}X_{j}-L\Big\|_{\psi_{p}}=0.

Further
\|L\|_{\psi_{p}}\leq\widetilde{C}_{\psi_{p}}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\|X_{j}\|_{\psi_{p}}
where
\widetilde{C}_{\psi_{p}}=1
for
p\geq 1
and is the constant
C_{\psi_{p}}
from Lemma
39
otherwise. When
X_{i}\geq 0
a.s., the sum
\sum_{j=1}^{N}X_{j}
also converges to
L
almost surely and in this case we will write
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}X_{j}
instead of
L
.

For completeness, the proof is provided below. We now present some technical results on limits and tail bounds that will are used extensively throughout the proofs.

Lemma 41
(Limits, Maxima, Independent sums, Tail bounds).

For all
i
, let
X_{i}
be a random variable.

(i)

If
\psi
is an Orlicz function,

\|\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}X_{n}\|_{\psi}\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|X_{n}\|_{\psi}.

(ii)

There exists a universal constant
K_{\Psi_{1}}
such that for
p>0

\Big\|\max_{1\leq i\leq K}X_{i}\Big\|_{\psi_{p}}\leq K_{\Psi_{1}}^{\frac{1}{p}}\log^{\frac{1}{p}}(K+1)\max_{i}\|X_{i}\|_{\psi_{p}}.

(iii)

Let
S_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
and suppose the
X_{i}
are independent and centered. For each
0<p\leq 2
,

\|S_{n}/\sqrt{n}\|_{\psi_{p}}\leq K_{p}\max_{i}\|X_{i}\|_{\psi_{p}},

for a universal constant
K_{p}
.

(iv)

For all
t\geq 0,p>0
,

{\mathrm{P}}(|X|\geq t)\leq 2\exp\Big(-\Big(\frac{t}{\|X\|_{\psi_{p}}}\Big)^{p}\Big).

The proofs of part (i) and (iii) of the above Lemma are given below for completeness. Part (iv) follows from the Markov inequality, part (ii) with an unspecified constant is given in Lemma 2.2.2 of
Van Der Vaart and
Wellner 2023
, a derivation with this form of constant can be found in
Decker 2024
(see Lemma H.0.4).

Lemma 42
(Relationships between quasi-norms).

Let
p,q>0
be real numbers, and let
X
and
Y
be a random variables.

(i)

\|X\|_{\psi_{p}}=\||X|^{p/q}\|_{\psi_{q}}^{q/p}
and
\|XY\|_{\psi_{p}}\leq\|X\|_{\psi_{2p}}\|Y\|_{\psi_{2p}}
.

(ii)

For any
p,q>0
there exists a universal constant
C_{p,q}
such that
\|X\|_{L_{q}}\leq C_{p,q}\|X\|_{\psi_{p}}
.

The proofs of both statements of part (i) in case
p=1
and
q=2
are given in Lemma 2.7.7 and 2.7.6 of
Vershynin 2018
. A proof in the general case is given below.

Lemma 43
.

Suppose
X(\omega,t)
is a measurable stochastic process with sample paths that are a.s. Riemann integrable. Suppose that
\|X(\cdot,t)\|_{\psi}\leq h(t)
for a Riemann integrable function
h
. Let
f(\omega)=\int_{I}X(\omega,t)dt
. Then for any Orlicz function
\psi
,
\|f(\omega)\|_{\psi}\leq\int_{I}h(t)dt
.

A more general version of this results involving Bochner integrals instead of Riemann integrals can be found in
Decker 2024
(see Lemma H.0.6).

All results discussed above are immediately generalized to conditional versions under the simple dependence structure assumed in the following definition. A more general definition of conditional Orlicz norm in terms of conditional expectation is possible, but not required in this article.

Definition 5
.

(Conditional Orlicz Norm)
Let
X
and
Y
be independent random elements taking values in separable metric spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
respectively Let
g:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be measurable. Then

\big[\|g(X,Y)\|_{\psi|\sigma(Y)}\big](\omega):=h(Y(\omega))\quad\mbox{for}\quad h(y):=\|g(X,y)\|_{\psi}.

(159)

Remark 8
.

Definition
5
allows the results of Lemma
41
and Lemma
42
to be generalized to the conditional case. For example, for each
y\in\mathcal{Y}
, let
f(y,t):={\mathrm{P}}\Big(g\big(X,y\big)\geq t\Big)
.
By Lemma
41
(iv) we have for all
t\geq 0
and any
p>0
,
f(y,t)\leq 2\exp\Big(-\Big(\frac{t}{\|g(X,y)\|_{\psi_{p}}}\Big)^{p}\Big)

and so

{\mathrm{P}}\Big(g\big(X,Y\big)\geq t\mid Y\Big)=f(Y,t)\leq 2\exp\Big(-\Big(\frac{t}{\|g(X,Y)\|_{\psi|\sigma(Y)}}\Big)^{p}\Big),

where all inequalities are almost sure and we used (
159
).

S.6.13
Proofs for section
S.6.12

Proof of Lemma
40

If
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\|X_{j}\|_{\psi_{p}}<\infty
, by Lemma
39
,
S_{M}=\sum_{j=1}^{M}X_{j}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{\widetilde{\psi}_{p}}
. By the same lemma,
\widetilde{\psi}_{p}
is an Orlicz function, and so by Lemma
38
,
S_{M}
converges in
L^{\widetilde{\psi}_{p}}
to a limit
L
satisfying
L\in L^{\widetilde{\psi}_{p}}
. By Lemma
39
,
L\in L^{{\psi}_{p}}
and the convergence also takes place in
L^{{\psi}_{p}}
. This proves the first claim. For the second claim, observe that

\Big|\|S_{M}\|_{\widetilde{\psi}_{p}}-\|L\|_{\widetilde{\psi}_{p}}\Big|\leq\Big\|S_{M}-L\Big\|_{\widetilde{\psi}_{p}}

by the reverse triangle inequality applied in the normed vector space
L^{\widetilde{\psi}_{p}}
. Fix
\varepsilon>0
. Since
S_{M}\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\psi}_{p}}L
,
\|S_{M}-L\|_{\widetilde{\psi}_{p}}\leq\varepsilon
for all sufficiently large
M
. Thus,

\|L\|_{\widetilde{\psi}_{p}}\leq\|S_{M}\|_{\widetilde{\psi}_{p}}+\varepsilon\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\|X_{j}\|_{\widetilde{\psi}_{p}}+\varepsilon.

Since
\varepsilon
was arbitrary,
\|L\|_{\widetilde{\psi}_{p}}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\|X_{j}\|_{\widetilde{\psi}_{p}}
. By Lemma
39
again, this implies the bound in the statement of the lemma we are proving. When
X_{j}\geq 0
a.s., the sum
\sum_{j=1}^{N}X_{j}(\omega)
is non-decreasing for almost all
\omega
and hence converges almost surely. The limit is a.s. finite and equal to
L
by uniqueness of limits in law.
\Box

Proof of Lemma
41
(i)

Without loss of generality,
\lim\inf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|X_{n}\|_{\psi}<\infty
, so let
K
be any strict upper bound. By Definition
4
,
\mathbb{E}[\psi(\frac{X_{n}}{K})]\leq 1
for all large
n
.
By continuity of the convex function
\psi
, Fatou’s Lemma, monotonicity of
\psi
, and the previous observation,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\psi\Big(\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\inf_{k\geq n}\frac{X_{n}}{K}\Big)\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\psi\Big(\inf_{k\geq n}\frac{X_{n}}{K}\Big)\Big]\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\psi\Big(\inf_{k\geq n}\frac{X_{n}}{K}\Big)\Big]\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\psi\Big(\frac{X_{n}}{K}\Big)\Big]\leq 1.

Thus by Definition
4
,

\Big\|\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}X_{n}\Big\|_{\psi}\leq\inf_{K}\Big\{K>\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|X_{n}\|_{\psi}\Big\}=\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\|X_{n}\|_{\psi}.

\Box

Proof of Lemma
41
, (iii)

First consider the case
0<p\leq 1
. By Proposition A.1.6 in
Van Der Vaart and
Wellner 2023
, originally proved in
Ledoux and
Talagrand 2013
as Theorem 6.21, there exists a constant
K_{p}
such that

\|S_{n}\|_{\psi_{p}}\leq K_{p}\Big(\mathbb{E}|S_{n}|+\|\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|X_{i}|\|_{\psi_{p}}\Big).

By Lemma
41
(ii),

\Big\|\max_{1\leq i\leq n}X_{i}\Big\|_{\psi_{p}}\leq K_{\Psi_{1}}^{\frac{1}{p}}\log^{1/p}(n+1)\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\|X_{i}\|_{\psi_{p}}.

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle\|S_{n}\|_{\psi_{p}}

\displaystyle\leq K_{p}\Big(\{\mathbb{E}[|S_{n}|^{2}]\}^{1/2}+K_{\Psi_{1}}^{\frac{1}{p}}\log^{1/p}(n+1)\max_{i}\|X_{i}\|_{\psi_{p}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\Big(\sqrt{n}\max_{i}\|X_{i}\|_{L_{2}}+\sqrt{n}\max_{i}\|X_{i}\|_{\psi_{p}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{p}\max_{i}\sqrt{n}\|X_{i}\|_{\psi_{p}},

where the value of the constant
C_{p}
can change from line to line. In the last line we applied Lemma
42
(ii). The proof in case case
p>1
follows from A.1.6 in
Van Der Vaart and
Wellner 2023
in a similar way.
\Box

Proof of Lemma
42
, (i)

To show the first statement in (i), we will first show that
\||X|^{p}\|_{\Psi_{1}}=\|X\|_{\psi_{p}}^{p}
. The general case will follow from this. Pick
B>\|X\|_{\psi_{p}}
. It follows that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\Big(\Big|\frac{|X|^{p}}{B^{p}}\Big|\Big)\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\Big(\Big|\frac{|X|}{B}\Big|^{p}\Big)\Big]\leq 2.

(160)

Thus
B^{p}\geq\||X|^{p}\|_{\Psi_{1}}
. Let
B_{n}\downarrow\|X\|_{\psi_{p}}
. By Fatou’s Lemma, and continuity of both
\exp\circ|\cdot|
and
w\rightarrow w^{p}
,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\Big(\Big|\frac{|X|^{p}}{\|X\|_{\psi_{p}}^{p}}\Big|\Big)\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\exp\Big(\Big|\frac{|X|^{p}}{B_{n}^{p}}\Big|\Big)\Big]\leq\liminf_{n\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\Big(\Big|\frac{|X|^{p}}{B_{n}^{p}}\Big|\Big)\Big]\leq 2.

(161)

Thus,
\||X|^{p}\|_{\Psi_{1}}\leq\|X\|_{\psi_{p}}^{p}
. The reverse inequality is obtained similarly: Let
B>\||X|^{p}\|_{\Psi_{1}}
, and note that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\Big(\Big|\Big(\frac{X}{B^{\frac{1}{p}}}\Big)\Big|^{p}\Big)\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\Big(\Big|\frac{|X|^{p}}{B}\Big|\Big)\Big]\leq 2.

(162)

An application of Fatou’s Lemma then shows that
\|X\|_{\psi_{p}}^{p}\leq\||X|^{p}\|_{\Psi_{1}}
.
The general result follows by noting that

\|X\|_{\psi_{p}}=\||X|^{p}\|_{\Psi_{1}}^{\frac{1}{p}}=\||X|^{q\frac{p}{q}}\|_{\Psi_{1}}^{\frac{1}{p}}=\||X|^{\frac{p}{q}}\|_{\psi_{q}}^{\frac{q}{p}}.

(163)

To show the second statement in item (i),we will first show that
\|XY\|_{\Psi_{1}}\leq\|Y\|_{\psi_{2}}\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}
. The general result will follow from since by the first half of part (i)

\|XY\|_{\psi_{p}}=\||XY|^{p}\|^{\frac{1}{p}}_{\Psi_{1}}\leq\||X|^{p}\|_{\psi_{2}}^{\frac{1}{p}}\||Y|^{p}\|_{\psi_{2}}^{\frac{1}{p}}=\|X\|_{\psi_{2p}}\|Y\|_{\psi_{2p}}.

To show
\|XY\|_{\Psi_{1}}\leq\|Y\|_{\psi_{2}}\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}
we may assume that
\|Y\|_{\psi_{2}}=\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}=1
since both sides are absolutely homogeneous with respect to multiplication by positive constants.

Let
B_{1}>1
and let
B_{2}>1
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\Big(\frac{|XY|}{(B_{1}B_{2})^{2}}\Big)\Big]\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\Big(\frac{|X|^{2}+|Y|^{2}}{2(B_{1}B_{2})^{2}}\Big)\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\Big(\Big|\frac{Y}{\sqrt{2}B_{1}B_{2}}\Big|^{2}\Big)\exp\Big(\Big|\frac{X}{\sqrt{2}B_{1}B_{2}}\Big|^{2}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\Big(\Big|\frac{Y}{B_{1}B_{2}}\Big|^{2}\Big)\Big]\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\Big(\Big|\frac{X}{B_{1}B_{2}}\Big|^{2}\Big)\Big]\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq 2.

Choosing sequences
B_{1,n}
and
B_{2,n}
that each converge downwards to 1, along with Fatou’s Lemma, finishes the proof.
\Box

Proof of Lemma
42
, (ii)

Let
B>\|X\|_{\psi_{p}}
. Then
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}[(|\frac{X}{B}|^{p})^{j}]}{j!}\leq 1
, and so for all natural numbers
j
,

\mathbb{E}[|X|^{pj}]\leq B^{pj}j!.

Let
j=\textrm{ceil}(\frac{q}{p})
, so that
pj\geq q
. By Jensen’s inequality,

\mathbb{E}[|X|^{q}]^{pj/q}\leq\mathbb{E}[|X|^{pj}].

These two inequalities combine to give

\mathbb{E}[|X|^{q}]^{1/q}\leq\mathbb{E}[|X|^{pj}]^{\frac{1}{pj}}\leq B(j!)^{\frac{1}{pj}}.

Set
C_{p,q}=(j!)^{1/pj}
. Note that
\textrm{ceil}(\frac{q}{p})\leq\frac{q}{p}+1
, and so
C_{p,q}\leq\Gamma(\frac{q}{p}+2)^{\frac{1}{q}}
.
\Box

Proof of Lemma
43

The following result is proved in
Spivak 2006
:

Lemma 44
.

If
f
is Riemann integrable on
[a,b]
, then for every
\varepsilon>0
there is some
\delta_{f,\varepsilon}>0
such that if
P=\{0,t_{1},\dots,1\}
is any partition of
[a,b]
with all lengths
t_{i}-t_{i-1}<\delta
, then

\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(x_{i})(t_{i}-t_{i-1})-\int_{[0,1]}f(x)dx\Big|\leq\varepsilon,

for any Riemann sum formed by choosing
x_{i}\in[t_{i-1},t_{i}]
.

We are now ready to prove the main statement. Let

f_{N}(\omega)=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}X(\omega,i/N).

Then
f_{N}(\omega)\rightarrow f(\omega)
whenever the sample path corresponding to
\omega
is Riemann integrable. Since this is assumed to be the case almost surely (by Lemma
44
),
f_{N}\rightarrow f
a.s. By the triangle inequality for the
\psi
norm and Lemma
41
(i),

\displaystyle\Big\|\int_{[0,1]}X(\cdot,t)dt\Big\|_{\psi}

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{N}\Big\|N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}X(\cdot,i/N)\Big\|_{\psi}

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{N}N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|X(\cdot,i/N)\|_{\psi}

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{N}N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}h(i/N)

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1]}h(t)dt.

The first inequality follows from Lemma
41
(i), the second inequality from the triangle inequality, and the final equality from the fact that,
\sum_{i=1}^{N}h(i/N)
is a right Riemann sum for the function
h(t)
and so (like any other Riemann sum) converges to
\int_{[0,1]}h(t)dt
.

\Box

Lemma 45
.

Let
Z=[X,Y]^{\top}
be a centered multivariate Gaussian such that
X\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
and
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
and
X
and
Y
are absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure. If for some orthogonal linear map
O
,
X=OY
,
{\mathrm{P}}(\|X\|_{2}=\|Y\|_{2})=1
. Otherwise,
{\mathrm{P}}(\|X\|_{2}=\|Y\|_{2})=0
.

Proof of Lemma
45

It is well known that for each
y\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
, we have
\textrm{law}\{X|Y=y\}=\mu(y)+N(0,\Sigma_{Z})
, where
\Sigma_{Z}:=\Sigma_{XX}-\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{YY}^{-1}\Sigma_{YX}
, for
\Sigma_{XX}:=\Var(X)
,
\Sigma_{YY}:=\Var(Y)
, and
\Sigma_{XY}:=\Cov(X,Y)
, and
\mu(y):=\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{YY}^{-1}y
. Therefore,

{\mathrm{P}}(\|X\|=\|Y\|)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\|X\|=\|y\|\penalty\ \Big|\penalty\ Y=y\Big)d{\mathrm{P}}^{Y}(y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\|\mu(y)+N(0,\Sigma_{Z})\|=\|y\|\Big)d{\mathrm{P}}^{Y}(y).

If
\Sigma_{Z}
is not the zero-matrix, we have for every
y\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
,

{\mathrm{P}}\Big(\|\mu(y)+N(0,\Sigma_{Z})\|=\|y\|\Big)=0.

If
\Sigma_{Z}
is the zero-matrix, we have for all
y
,
\textrm{law}\{X|Y=y\}=\Sigma_{XY}\Sigma_{YY}^{-1}y
, and so
X=AY
, almost surely, for
A=\Sigma_{XY}[\Sigma_{YY}]^{-1}
. If
A
is orthogonal, we have

{\mathrm{P}}(\|X\|_{2}=\|Y\|_{2})={\mathrm{P}}(\|AY\|_{2}=\|Y\|_{2})={\mathrm{P}}(\|Y\|_{2}=\|Y\|_{2})=1.

Now assume that
A
is not orthogonal. Note that
\|Ay\|^{2}=y^{\top}A^{\top}Ay
. The matrix
A^{\top}A
is symmetric, thus there exists an orthonormal basis, say
v_{1},\dots,v_{d}
, of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
of eigenvectors of
A^{\top}A
. Let
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{d}
denote the corresponding eigenvalues. Then for
y=\sum\beta_{i}v_{i}
we have
\|Ay\|^{2}=\|y\|^{2}
if and only if
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\beta_{i}^{2}=\sum\beta_{i}^{2}
. The set of
\beta_{i}
which satisfy this is a Lebesgue null set unless all
\lambda_{i}=1
. Indeed, if say
\lambda_{1}\neq 1
then
\beta_{1}^{2}=-\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}-1}\sum_{i\neq 1}(\lambda_{i}-1)\beta_{i}^{2}
. Therefore, the set
\{\|Ay\|^{2}=y^{\top}A^{\top}Ay\}
is the countable union of the graphs smooth functions and so has Lebesgue measure zero.
\Box
